#3052. Where Will SoFi Stock Be in 2030? Its Goal Is 100 Million Products.

Look beyond the projected dollar amounts to better understand how this online bank is ensuring it's growing shareholder value.

The primary purpose of any for-profit company turning as much revenue as possible into as much profit as possible. How it intends to plausibly do that, however, is just as important to interested investors.

While online bank SoFi Technologies (SOFI -3.93%) celebrated its customer headcount growing 35% year over year to 17.1 million during the third quarter of 2026, that's not the only metric that matters. Neither are revenue growth of 44% (to $1.4 billion) or the 70% year-over-year improvement in EBITDA (to $470 million).

Just as important right now for SoFi is the number of individual products or services -- like brokerage accounts, savings, insurance, credit cards, etc. -- its customer base now uses. This number grew 44% to a record 26.7 million in Q3, as more than half of its current customers signed up for another service.

It matters because any company's most likely and most cost-effective sales prospect is an existing customer already in its ecosystem.

Don't misunderstand. Maximizing net income is still the ultimate goal. Clear, plausible pathways to reaching a goal, though, bolster the bullish case for any publicly traded outfit. This one is no exception.

And SoFi Technologies' growth outlook is not only compelling, but plausible.

Yes, the company's got longer-term revenue and earnings goals. For instance, SoFi Technologies is aiming to generate at least $8 billion in annual revenue by 2030, up from a little over $5 billion this year, and $6.4 billion in sales next year, $2.24 billion of which should be turned into EBITDA. And based on nothing more than its current fiscal trajectory, these are very achievable targets.

Data source: Morningstar. Chart by author.

This long-term outlook may actually understate what's in store, however, given SoFi's secret weapon hiding in plain sight. That's its existing customer base of 17.1 million customers, which has steadily grown from a headcount of just over 1 million as of the first quarter of 2020. As it turns out, this is a fantastic source of opportunities for continued growth.

CEO Anthony Noto made no bones about it during Q3's earnings conference call, explaining, "We see a clear path to achieving 100 million products by the end of 2030." That's a 4x increase over its total right now.

The company doesn't necessarily need to quadruple its membership base to reach that target, though. The longer SoFi Technologies remains in business and the longer its customers utilize the online bank's offerings, the more comfortable they become with the brand. That's why, as of Q3, every member now uses an average of 1.56 SoFi products, up from 1.47 a year earlier, and well up from 1.36 as of 2020.

Data source: SoFi Technologies. Chart by author.

Assuming the growth pace of both numbers remains in place until then, SoFi Technologies should have no problem reaching Noto's goal. Revenue and earnings are sure to follow suit.

Perhaps more important to interested investors, assuming the company can continue growing like it is, SOFI shares could conceivably at least double in value by 2030. Some of this potential growth is already priced in, but at only 18 times next year's projected per-share profit of $0.90, certainly much of it isn't.

#3051. Securing Microsoft Identities At Agentic Speed

Robert Bobel, founder & CEO of Cayosoft, is dedicated to helping organizations succeed by modernizing IT with innovative hybrid technologies

Imagine a presentation to an IT team showing an AI agent completing an administrative task in seconds. The demonstration works, the team sees the potential to scale, and someone asks how soon the agent can go into production. Before that happens, someone should ask what else the agent has permission to do, whether it can be constrained through a least-privilege delegation model and how the organization will recover if that authority is misused.

The biggest risk is not simply what an agent can do, but what the organization may authorize it to do without fully understanding the consequences. An agent’s assigned task and its broader access can be very different things. If the task is narrow but the permissions are broad, the organization has authorized far more than the business requested.

For enterprises built on Microsoft infrastructure, this is an immediate identity security concern. Agents may interact with Microsoft 365 data, cloud services and administrative workflows connected to Active Directory, impacting network resources and user identities.

The efficiency gains from AI are compelling, but organizations should be equally impatient about securing the infrastructure that makes those gains possible. If the Hugging Face incident demonstrated anything, it is that every decision to expand an agent’s reach should come with a clear decision about its limits.

Agents can present an identity threat in two ways. First, attackers can use them to automate steps in an intrusion. Second, legitimate agents can become a route to sensitive systems if their credentials are stolen or their actions are manipulated. Organizations need to prepare for both.

Neither scenario requires AI to break through every defense. An attacker who obtains a credential with excessive privileges already has an opportunity. Automation can exploit that opportunity with less manual effort. A compromised internal agent presents a related problem because it may already have permission to perform useful and consequential work.

Microsoft’s guidance on securing autonomous agents makes the identity connection explicit. It calls for unique, traceable agent identities and access that is limited in scope and duration, with the ability to revoke it.

Organizations can act on the permissions they grant today, examine the systems those permissions reach and decide which actions carry too much business risk to run without additional checks. They should also determine how they will identify and reverse consequential changes that occur before access is revoked.

Before giving an agent access, organizations need to examine the work it will perform and the authority each step requires. These six practices can establish clear boundaries and maintain them as the deployment evolves.

Consider an agent intended to help with employee onboarding. Reading an approved request, creating an account and assigning access are separate decisions. Successfully reading the request should not automatically authorize every subsequent action. Each step needs an appropriate permission boundary.

The owner should be able to explain the agent’s purpose and approve access changes. Organizations should also review what the agent can reach across connected systems. An agent should not inherit a broad administrative account simply because that account makes integration easier.

This is particularly important in hybrid Microsoft environments, where a cloud workflow may rely on an identity or service that also has responsibilities on-premises. Reviewing one application in isolation can miss the consequences of that relationship. Access needs to be evaluated end to end, including the combined authority created by the workflow.

Routine work within a narrow scope can proceed automatically. Granting elevated privileges or making widespread administrative changes deserves a check before execution.

Ask the team to demonstrate that the agent cannot make an unauthorized change, even when instructed to do so. Demonstrating what the agent is prevented from doing can be as valuable as demonstrating what it accomplishes.

Security teams need to connect consequential changes to the identity responsible and understand whether its access has expanded. A new integration should trigger another review. Otherwise, an agent approved for a modest task can gradually acquire responsibilities that nobody has evaluated together.

Agentic speed also changes what organizations should expect from incident response. A process that depends on finding the right administrator, explaining the situation and determining how to revoke access introduces unnecessary delay. The procedure should be established in advance and tested to confirm that it stops activity across connected systems.

Containing the activity is only part of the response. The changes already made still need to be addressed. If an identity was granted inappropriate access, can the team identify and reverse it? If an incident broadly damages Active Directory, can the organization restore it to a trustworthy state?

Recovery belongs in the AI deployment conversation from the beginning because identity supports the business long after the demonstration ends. Applications and employees depend on it to work. When that infrastructure fails, the consequences can extend well beyond the agent that initiated the incident. A recovery exercise should establish what the team can restore, how the result will be validated and how long restoration takes, including what happens when normal administrative access is unavailable.

Organizations have every reason to pursue the productivity that agents can deliver. AI can reduce manual effort and help people act on what matters, but consequential decisions still require human judgment. That ambition needs to come with an investment in identity security and resilience. The people responsible for Microsoft infrastructure need to be involved while access decisions are being made, with enough time to test the controls and recovery procedures.

Before approving the next agent for production, ask for evidence of its limits and a demonstration of how the business will regain control and restore trust if something goes wrong. That is how organizations earn the confidence to let agents act.

#3049. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The ongoing earnings season, investor concerns about the durability of AI demand and spending, and geopolitical risks are key factors that have been contributing to stock market volatility in recent trading sessions.

In this scenario, investors seeking steady income can consider adding dividend stocks to their portfolios. Recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help them pick attractive dividend stocks that are backed by solid cash flows to support consistent payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips is this week's first dividend pick. With a dividend of 84 cents per share (annualized dividend of $3.36 per share), COP offers a dividend yield of 3%. The company is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on Aug. 6.

Ahead of second-quarter results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin reiterated a buy rating on COP stock with a price target of $183. Despite the pressure on oil prices from an increase in OPEC production quota, the analyst finds ConocoPhillips and Shell stocks appealing as the earnings season approaches. He cited their operational visibility and resilience as factors backing their appeal.

The 5-star analyst expects ConocoPhillips to meet its production guidance of 2.2 million barrels of oil equivalent per day at the mid-point. He expects lower Waha natural gas prices in the Permian Basin to be offset by stronger Brent crude premiums. Margolin expects capital expenditure to remain within COP's prior guided range of $12.2 billion annualized, with no significant impact on spending on the Northfield East project in Qatar despite the Strait of Hormuz disruption.

Overall, Margolin expects COP to generate about $3.5 billion in free cash flow (before working capital) and earnings per share of $2.94. He expects continued strength in COP's free cash flow and regular dividend growth through the completion of the Willow project in 2028/2029. Prior to the Willow project coming online, the analyst expects free cash flow to grow by about $2 billion in 2027 and 2028, assuming Brent crude averages around $60 per barrel.

"COP's track record of capital efficiency and strong Permian well productivity underpins its ability to pursue long-cycle developments," said Margolin.

Margolin ranks No. 457 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 70% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.3%. See ConocoPhillips Financials on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer is a limited partnership that operates 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. With a quarterly cash distribution of 33.75 cents per common unit ($1.35 per unit on an annualized basis), ET offers a yield of 6.8%.

Heading into Energy Transfer's Q2 earnings on Aug. 4, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith reaffirmed a buy rating on ET stock with a price target of $23. The analyst noted that his adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization estimate of $4.46 billion is 1% below the Street's consensus of $4.49 billion.

The 5-star analyst noted that Energy Transfer has slightly outperformed Enterprise Products Partners recently. However, it still trades at a relative discount of 19% compared to EPD, which is below its historical discount range of 17%-20%. Smith believes that ET stock could be re-rated higher if the company provides a clearer long-term strategy for natural gas growth.

Furthermore, Smith expects the current energy market to support a stronger outlook for natural gas liquids and crude oil. "The current energy macro backdrop positions ET to benefit in all three commodities," said the analyst.

He expects Energy Transfer's adjusted EBITDA to grow at a 4.8% compound annual growth rate in 2027-2030, which is 1%-3% above Wall Street's expectations. In fact, Smith sees the possibility of additional upside if ET announces more natural gas projects. He added that investors will await details on final investment decisions on new natural gas projects and any clues about additional projects in the pipeline. The analyst noted that ET has announced new gas projects consistently in recent quarters.

Smith ranks No. 550 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.4%. See Energy Transfer Statistics on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at energy giant Chevron, which is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on July 31. Last month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12, CVX offers a dividend yield of 3.92%.

Ahead of Q2 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on Chevron stock and lowered his price target to $216 from $236. Byrne expects the company to report adjusted EPS of about $5.86 per share, nearly 9% above the Street's expectations.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the challenges seen in Chevron's upstream business in the first quarter due to the disruption at the Tengizchevroil joint venture in Kazakhstan, Storm Fern downtime, and the Middle East conflict have largely been resolved. Consequently, Byrne expects production to recover in the second quarter to about 4,033 mboepd. He expects the upstream business to generate adjusted earnings of about $8.1 billion in Q2 2026.

Meanwhile, Byrne expects Chevron to generate downstream adjusted earnings of about $4.4 billion in Q2, with strength in both domestic and international markets. The downstream business benefited from higher crack spreads and strong refining performance.

Additionally, the analyst expects Chevron to generate $18.2 billion in cash flow from operations (before working capital changes), driven by stronger earnings and about $2.2 billion in dividends from affiliated companies. Unlike the first quarter, Chevron is not expected to make a TCO loan repayment in Q2, providing an additional boost to cash flow.

Byrne ranks No. 409 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 56% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.5%. See Chevron Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3048. Codere Online: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Spain's new deposit rules add near-term friction, yet stable acquisition costs show marketing spend can grow without eroding profitability.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 12, 2026, Codere Online (CDRO -1.56%) announced revenue grew 31% year over year to 62.6 million euros, driven by expanding player spend. Online gaming operators depend on converting user volume into profitable retention rather than burning capital on low-value users. Managing player value determines whether top-line expansion generates durable earnings. Three things management said stand out.

Management prioritized player quality over mass volume, lifting average monthly spend per active user 24% to 134 euros. Higher spend per user translates directly into improved profitability without forcing the business to overspend on customer acquisition.

Spain introduced new identification and deposit rules that apply across operators, creating temporary operational hurdles. Established companies with proper infrastructure can adapt while smaller competitors struggle to comply.

Management kept cost per acquisition, or CPA, which measures the marketing expense required to sign up a new paying player, steady at 196 euros over the last 12 months. Maintaining stable acquisition costs lets the business protect adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a measure of core operating profitability, even as total marketing spend expands.

Revenue momentum continues across key markets like Mexico, and leadership remains comfortable aiming for the upper end of its full-year targets. The business holds structural advantages in its debt-free balance sheet and negative working capital model, while management closely watches regulatory developments, and foreign currency fluctuations.

Looking beyond near-term regulatory adjustments in Europe, leadership intends to keep its capital allocation framework grounded in measured spending and disciplined underwriting.

#3046. How Much Is Nvidia Stock Worth Without Jensen Huang?

Nvidia (NVDA -4.34%) has been a surefire winner for investors in recent years. The artificial intelligence (AI) stock soared more than 1,300% from the start of 2023 through the end of October. This is thanks to the company's top AI chips and its charismatic leader, Jensen Huang.

Over the past few days, however, Nvidia has experienced a reversal of fortune. Allegations emerged of affairs concerning Huang -- one alleged affair being with a person U.S. intelligence says is a Chinese spy. Nvidia's board opened an investigation, then met to discuss the company's future, and Huang subsequently resigned.

Now investors are asking: How much is Nvidia stock worth without Jensen Huang at the helm? Let's find out.

First, let's consider the recent turmoil in more detail. Last weekend, Nvidia's board announced an investigation "in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort."

In an email to employees obtained by CNBC, Huang apologized for being unfaithful to his wife and expressed his commitment to Nvidia and his willingness to cooperate with the investigation.

Days later, The New York Times reported that Huang also had an affair with Nvidia executive Kimberly Powell. Nvidia's board called a meeting to discuss the company's future, and Huang resigned early Thursday morning. The board named Colette Kress, Nvidia's chief financial officer, as interim CEO and will search internally and externally for a permanent leader.

Nvidia fell a little more than 4% in the Thursday trading session following the news, bringing the stock to a 10% decline so far this year. This is a sharp departure from the stock's performance over the past few years. As mentioned above, it's roared higher and has been the "go-to" AI stock for many investors. Nvidia's market capitalization even reached beyond $5 trillion at its peak, securing its position as the biggest company.

Now, let's consider how much Nvidia stock is worth without Jensen Huang in charge. Huang offered a significant amount of value since he not only was the founder, but he maintained a solid position in the driver's seat, with 60 employees reporting directly to him. He was seen as a spokesperson for AI around the world, considering his company's AI chip dominance and his focus on innovation. And the world looked to Huang for clues about the next chapters of AI growth.

Nvidia stock closed at about $166 on Nov. 12. Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives and analysts at Citi see the value of the stock at $225 without Jensen Huang. This is down from $300 for Ives and $285 from Citi. So this level still implies a gain from today's price. It's important to note that Nvidia is historically cheap relative to its earnings today, trading at only 21x trailing 12-month earnings.

Chip contracts are set months in advance, and Nvidia's innovation wheels are turning, with updates planned and teams working on the technology. So there's reason to be optimistic about the company's products and earnings over the next few quarters. And that's why the stock's value could continue to advance from here.

But significant risks remain. Investors might question whether the permanent CEO, once named, will have the vision and strategy to keep Nvidia in its leading position in the AI market. Meanwhile, in the near term, Nvidia may come under deeper government scrutiny and could even face renewed export restrictions to China. A Senate committee summoned Huang to testify next month regarding his alleged relationship with the woman U.S. intelligence is calling a spy. Any information from such testimony could have implications for Nvidia.

Considering Nvidia's well-established technology and market position, as well as the placement of Kress, who knows the company well, in the driver's seat, Nvidia could continue to advance toward $225. But the path may be choppy until the uncertainties have lifted and a permanent CEO establishes a clear strategy.

#3044. Application Bloat Is An Emergency Waiting To Happen

I recently spoke with an IT leader from a large hospital. He described how he had recently renewed an application software contract, only to learn that the hospital’s corporate parent health system had also just purchased a different platform for the same function.​ The IT leader had no idea the enterprise deal was coming. And now he was stuck with a six-figure annual contract for an IT solution he would likely never even use. ​

Unfortunately, this disconnect is all too common, where the left hand is not talking to the right hand. These communication gaps, or failure to align applications with business needs, are costing health systems more than they realize. It’s a category of waste that is almost entirely preventable, but avoiding it requires IT and finance leaders to establish a shared view of what the organization owns, what it costs and which systems should remain part of its future. That’s called an application portfolio modernization (APM) analysis.​

A thorough APM engagement is different from a simple application inventory. Where an inventory tells you what applications you own, APM goes further, identifying systems that duplicate each other and telling you how much that duplication is costing the organization. It can also recommend which solution should become the standard. ​

Tools already exist to help business leaders undertake the APM process. Gartner’s TIME framework helps identify which systems are active and determine how much each application costs. It helps business stakeholders create a strategic plan outlining which systems should be Tolerated, Invested, Migrated or Eliminated (TIME).​

The financial case for APM is obvious. According to a 2026 survey from The College of Healthcare Information Management Executives (CHIME), an executive organization dedicated to serving digital health leaders, 76% of CIOs say that application rationalization is critical to their application strategies, with more than one-third calling it a major driver of cost savings. Yet only one in five say they have a fully implemented, ongoing program, and 20% of the CIOs surveyed say their organizations have not even started the work.

It’s confounding to me why something so critical keeps falling down the list of priorities, and why IT and finance leaders so rarely come together to aggressively pursue the savings. Perhaps part of the problem is that these expenses are already baked into annual budgets and spread across departments. IT may see the duplication but lack the capacity or authority to retire the systems behind it because the business stakeholders don’t have an enterprise-level alignment of their software portfolio. Finance, meanwhile, may see the spending but not realize how much could be saved if IT and the business aligned on which systems to keep.

Without a shared mandate, those costs roll forward year after year (after year). Each additional merger and acquisition, cloud migration and enterprise application rollout can add another layer of expense when the systems they could replace remain in place. Over time, health systems can accumulate a mountain of technical debt, paying to maintain the past while reducing innovations for the future, or worse, causing unsustainable operating losses.

I think many of us behave similarly in our own lives. If we’re suddenly confronted with an unexpected $5,000 car repair, that becomes an urgent priority. Have you recently reviewed your cell phone app subscriptions or your streaming subscriptions? When our expenses slowly rise over time due to adding streaming subscriptions, unused memberships and rising insurance premiums, we often don’t take much notice. When the extra costs do catch our attention, we tend to procrastinate, pushing reconciliation and consolidation to another, more convenient time that never seems to come.

Application portfolios get bloated in much the same way, though the associated risks and costs accumulate at a much greater scale. Organizations acquire companies, add platforms, replace systems and purchase specialized tools, with new applications always seeming to arrive much faster than old ones get retired.

We need to reframe how we think about the costs of application bloat. Money tied up in redundant systems is money unavailable to retain experienced staff, support patient care or invest in AI and other innovations. CIOs and CFOs need to look beyond whether software spending stays within budget and ask strategically what that spending is costing the organization in people and capabilities.

Think of that IT leader locked into a six-figure annual contract for a system his hospital would never use. Those dollars could help keep a valued employee on the payroll. Multiply that by dozens or hundreds of cases across years of acquisitions, cloud migrations and application replacements, and the consequences become much harder to dismiss.

Ongoing reductions in force (RIFs) across healthcare put that tradeoff into sharper focus. Hospitals and health systems are eliminating jobs as they confront mounting financial pressures. Every position lost represents a person’s livelihood, along with experience and institutional knowledge the organization may struggle to replace.

Application portfolio modernization will not prevent every layoff or fund each new innovation. But eliminating unnecessary recurring costs can certainly reduce the savings leaders need to find elsewhere, including through workforce cuts.

CIOs and CFOs should make application portfolio modernization a shared priority before financial pressures force decisions about staffing. Eliminating redundant systems creates recurring savings that can help preserve jobs and the expertise those employees bring. Before we decide we can no longer afford the people doing essential work, we should ask why we’re still paying for applications that aren’t.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3043. This Media Stock Has a $1 Billion Market Cap. A Pending Lawsuit Could Reap as Much as $2.7 Billion in Damages.

The largest news publisher in the country has sued Google over monopolistic advertising practices that it believes cost the company billions.

Few industries have felt disruption in the 21st century more than the news business. The industry is still adapting to the disruption of the internet, which killed profitable parts of the business, such as the classifieds section, and made the print business an expensive, antiquated endeavor that is likely on its way out.

Artificial intelligence has presented new challenges, particularly by changing the way people search for and get information. Because AI models often provide answers to queries right away, there is less need for consumers to click on articles, which is hurting page views and subscription conversions.

USA Today (TDAY -5.38%) has dealt with all of these challenges and then some. While the company is still figuring out how to run an independent news and content organization profitably, the stock has seen many ups and downs over the past five years.

Right now, it's at roughly a $1 billion market cap. A potential lawsuit it is undertaking could result in up to $2.7 billion in damages.

In 2019, New Media Investment Group, a conglomerate of regional and local newspapers and websites, merged with Gannett, the owner of USA Today, to form the country's largest newspaper company. At the time, New Media was the technical acquirer and valued Gannett at $1.4 billion, which shows how the stock has performed since.

As the world of news and content in general has shifted, USA Today, the name the company recently adopted, has tried to adapt, focusing on digital news subscriptions and digital advertising.

The company also has a digital marketing services business for small- and medium-sized businesses and now licenses its content to large artificial intelligence companies. Meanwhile, the company's print business and associated expenses continue to shrink.

In 2027, management hopes digital revenue will account for over 50% of total revenue. While the company has certainly made progress, investors remain cautious about whether the core business has yet to find a sustainable model. AI has thrown another wrinkle into the equation.

Over the years, news publishers have argued that Alphabet’s (GOOG +1.62%)(GOOGL +1.79%) Google has effectively held a monopoly in the online advertising market, making it difficult for them to negotiate prices and ad space and depriving them of revenue.

This argument picked up steam when the U.S. Department of Justice sued Google in 2023, alleging that its Ad Exchange "engaged in 15 years of sustained conduct that had -- and continues to have -- the effect of driving out rivals, diminishing competition, inflating advertising costs, reducing revenues for news publishers and content creators, snuffing out innovation, and harming the exchange of information and ideas in the public sphere."

A federal judge sided with the DOJ and ultimately required Google to change its advertising practices.

Being the largest news publisher in the country, USA Today has felt the impact of Google's practices and long complained about them. USA Today piggybacked on the DOJ's lawsuit and separately sued Google for similar monopolistic practices, seeking to restore competition in publishers' advertising.

Because a federal judge has already sided with the DOJ, the often lengthy litigation process has sped up. Recently, Google's motion for summary judgment was denied, allowing USA Today's and other publishers' claims to proceed to trial. USA Today's trial is slated to begin on April 5, 2027.

This ruling also revealed that USA Today estimates damages from Google's advertising practices at around $900 million through 2024. Not only does USA Today CEO Mike Reed expect to update this figure ahead of trial, but there is the potential for treble damages under U.S. antitrust law. That would be $2.7 billion based on the 2024 figure.

Another good sign is that USA Today is being represented by the prominent antitrust firm Kellogg Hansen, which has secured both the largest and second-largest antitrust legal victories in the U.S. The firm is also taking on the case on a contingency basis.

With trial set to begin in about five months, there's a real chance Google settles. Christine Bartholomew, a law professor at the University at Buffalo who specializes in antitrust, told Bloomberg Law that, statistically, most antitrust cases settle. That said, Google certainly has the resources to go to trial if it so chooses.

Sitting at a roughly $1 billion market cap, even a settlement in the hundreds of millions would make a significant difference. The 2019 merger resulted in USA Today taking on significant debt from Apollo Global Management.

When the deal closed, the combined entity had close to $1.8 billion of debt. USA Today has now reduced its debt to under $1 billion, although it has had to implement numerous cost-cutting measures that have been painful for its journalists.

With net debt of roughly $885 million, USA Today has an enterprise value of roughly $1.88 billion. Analysts at Citigroup recently estimated a $690 million settlement with Google. Assuming that comes to fruition and USA Today puts all or most of it toward paying down debt, which I assume management will do, that should result in a new market cap of nearly $1.7 billion, implying 70% upside for the stock in Citigroup's scenario, although some of the debt is convertible, meaning there could be dilution. The significant legal fees incurred will likely also lower the final funds available to reinvest.

Paying down significant debt would also remove a significant portion of the company's over-$20 million in quarterly interest expense, resulting in substantial earnings accretion. Finally, reducing the debt overhang could allow USA Today to invest more in the business and go on the offensive.

While things look good, investors shouldn't assume that Google will choose to settle, which could delay any potential damages. Google could also emerge victorious if it goes to trial. It's also very difficult to put a number on potential damages.

I'm bullish on the outcome of the lawsuit and own USA Today stock, but I would reiterate that there is a wide range of outcomes.

#3041. The Race To Produce Ads Faster Is A Race Toward The Middle

Tanmay Ratnaparkhe, Co-Founder, Predis.ai, which uses AI to help brands scale ads, ad videos and social content without losing their voice.

​The marketing teams I speak to all share the same point: They can now produce more advertisements in less time than they could a year ago. Work that once required a week of filming and revisions can now be done in an afternoon with a few prompts. The output figures look impressive. Yet when I ask whether the work is actually performing better, I usually get a pause.

What makes the pause so interesting is that for the past two years production speed has been our aim, and I now believe that we should have chosen a different target. Once anyone can produce 100 variations before lunch, speed stops being an advantage and becomes the basic standard. When everyone has the same basic standard, however, it no longer helps you to stand out.​

For nearly the entire history of advertising, the difficult part was production. Making advertisements was a slow, costly process, so the ability to produce more was important. That is no longer the case. The tools that enable my team to work quickly are generally the same ones all competitors have. Once an entire category can produce at the same speed, speed stops being the deciding factor.

The unpleasant thing is that the capability everyone has rushed to develop has little defensive value; if a competitor can match your output by Friday, then output was never really your advantage. Experience shows that teams that still assess themselves primarily on volume are most surprised when their performance levels off.​

A more fundamental reason the race tends everybody toward the middle is that this tendency is inherent in how the tools function. Generative models are trained to generate what is most statistically likely—the most probable following line, the most probable next arrangement. At their default settings, they move toward the average of all the examples they have already seen. This is not merely a guess. A meta-analysis of creative tasks in 2026 found a small but statistically significant effect of homogenization when people used generative AI, compared with when they didn’t. When given similar briefs and using similar tools, people produced similar results.

I can see this taking place in creative reviews. The initial ideas all seem original, but by the 20th they start to blend together—merely competent, fashionable and forgettable. The speed that once felt liberating gradually creates a house style no one actually chose. Faster iteration doesn’t help you avoid this problem; it only gets you there faster.​

If production isn’t the bottleneck, the scarce resource has shifted to judging which ideas are worth showing people. It’s now easy to come up with 50 options, but the difficulty lies in deciding which of them should actually receive a real budget, and having the ability to justify that decision. No tool will do that for you.

On my teams, the most valuable person isn’t the one who produces the most assets; it’s the one who, when faced with a multitude of options, can give a genuine reason for saying none of them feel like our brand. That sort of judgment—a clear understanding of what the brand is and what it isn’t—is something automation can’t provide. In my experience, it’s also what distinguishes work that gets carried forward from work that is simply scrolled past.​

The solution is not to reduce the speed. That speed is genuine and has a useful purpose. The issue then becomes what you choose to spend it on: You can use it to produce more of the same, or you can use it to make genuine attempts at being distinctive and discard the ones that fail.

As a result, the way we work changes. Rather than asking the tool for a hundred usable ads, I use the time that is saved to improve the brief, clarify the point of view and set a higher standard for what is good enough to run. You should treat generation as the cheap part and judgment as the expensive one, since that better reflects current costs. The teams I observe advancing aren’t the ones who produce the fastest. They are the ones most ready to discard work.

The competition to move faster will keep accelerating, and most of the industry will keep joining in. However, the result of this competition is the middle ground—where nothing really stands out. The more valuable race, and the one fewer teams take part in, is simply being worth noticing at all. In that race, the winner is not the person who produces the most; instead, the winner is the one who knows what is worth producing.

#3038. Top Wall Street analysts back these 3 stocks for their long-term growth potential

Major indexes remain volatile as investors assess key earnings releases and geopolitical tensions in the Middle East. Investors with a long-term horizon should look beyond the ongoing noise and focus on stocks that are well-positioned to generate strong returns.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist stocks with robust growth opportunities. These experts conduct an in-depth analysis of all the macro and micro factors affecting a company, focusing on its long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

CrowdStrike Holdings is a cloud-native cybersecurity provider. The company recently announced an expanded partnership with Schwarz Digits to bring its Falcon platform to European enterprises.

After hosting several European investor meetings with the CrowdStrike CFO, Stifel analyst Adam Borg reiterated a buy rating on CRWD stock and increased his price target to $230 from $220.

The five-star analyst said that the meetings increased his confidence about CrowdStrike being an "important, well-positioned cybersecurity platform vendor that is an AI beneficiary given its expansive portfolio, unique data set, and ability to both secure AI and use AI to improve security."

Among the key takeaways, Borg noted that AI is driving a major shift in cybersecurity demand as it presents software vulnerabilities that can be exploited by less sophisticated hackers with capabilities that were once limited to government-backed hacking groups. This has led to companies making cybersecurity a top priority, driving customer interest in CrowdStrike's new AI Detection & Response solution and its broader security platform.

Borg added that AI-driven demand is expanding CrowdStrike's pipeline and supporting confidence in the company's higher fiscal 2027 net new annual recurring revenue guidance issued last quarter. Overall, Borg believes that CrowdStrike has multiple growth drivers that can help maintain revenue growth in at least the high-teens and drive improved profitability in the years ahead.

Borg ranks No. 651 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See CrowdStrike Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving on to AST SpaceMobile, a company building a global cellular broadband network in space. Recently, Piper Sandler analyst Alexander Potter initiated coverage of space-related stocks. While the analyst assigned AST SpaceMobile a buy rating with a price target of $100, he has a neutral stance on rocket builders SpaceX and Rocket Lab due to valuation concerns.

"We prefer ASTS, due to a more palatable valuation, and a clearer path to EBITDA upside," said Potter.

The five-star analyst highlighted that AST SpaceMobile builds satellites that connect directly to smartphones, ensuring network connectivity for users everywhere and enabling applications like video calls, streaming, and gaming. Potter added that ASTS' technology helps Mobile Network Operators, or MNOs, expand coverage and offer add-on services to travelers and users in remote locations.

Potter ranks No. 708 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 48% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.2%. See AST SpaceMobile Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at Broadcom, a provider of custom AI chips and networking solutions. Recently, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on Broadcom stock with a price target of $502.

The five-star analyst is surprised by the underperformance of AVGO stock so far this year compared to its peers, especially given continued momentum in AI-driven demand. Moore cited concerns that competitor MediaTek could gain market share from Broadcom in Google's tensor processing unit chip business and investor preference for faster-growing AI chip names as reasons for the underperformance.

While Moore acknowledged that MediaTek's role in Google's TPU business is growing, he contends that it is unlikely to disrupt Broadcom's position significantly. He expects Broadcom to continue to be the major TPU supplier, with about 80% market share. Overall, Moore believes that concerns about a decline in AVGO's market share to 50% or its eventual replacement seem premature.

"AVGO should retain majority share, sustain strong AI growth, and remain a core compute winner, close behind NVDA," said Moore.

The analyst explained that his bullish outlook on Broadcom is based on the company's solid advantage in high-bandwidth memory supply, chip packaging capabilities, and large-scale production. Moore also highlighted AVGO's dominance in AI ASIC chips, strong networking business, and new customer wins.

Moore ranks No. 148 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 25.5%. See Broadcom Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

#3037. This Quip Attributed to Former Berkshire Hathaway CEO Warren Buffett Is Useful When Thinking About Nvidia's Future

Nvidia (NVDA -4.34%) is in a bit of a mess right now. Jensen Huang stepped down as president, chief executive officer, and a member of the board this week after reports of extramarital affairs surfaced, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. Some people view Huang as one of the primary reasons for investing in Nvidia stock.

However, there’s one investing gem attributed to one legendary CEO that is useful to ponder at this time. Former Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +0.09%) (BRKB +0.27%) CEO Warren Buffett has denied he ever said it, but he’s often credited with saying that it can make sense to invest in companies that a “ham sandwich” could lead, meaning partly that their success isn’t tied to any one person.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

Investors should look at every company they own and determine if the company could be successfully run by someone else, or if the current leader is integral to the company's success.

Two examples of a business that may not be able to be run by anyone else are Tesla and SpaceX. Both of these are run by CEO Elon Musk, who may be a controversial figure, but is seen by many as one of the brightest technological minds in the world.

Some may say the same thing about Huang, who was one of Nvidia's co-founders and had led the company from the start. That means he steered it to develop GPUs meant for data centers and AI, which allowed Nvidia to become the world's largest company at one time. He also oversaw Nvidia developing class-leading technology and maintaining its advantage over the competition as they improved their technology.

The question now that Huang has resigned is (with no offense to the current and future leaders of Nvidia): Could a ham sandwich run Nvidia, or does it require Jensen Huang at the helm to succeed?

At its peak, Nvidia's market cap was nearly $5.8 trillion. On Sunday, Nov. 8, Huang sent a note to Nvidia employees admitting “I was unfaithful to my wife.” On Wednesday, Nov. 11, reports were circulating that the company’s board was going to oust him. On Thursday, Nov. 12, his resignation was announced. On Friday morning, the stock had a market cap of $4 trillion.

That clearly conveys that the market doesn't believe a "ham sandwich" could run Nvidia. But is that a fair assessment?

Nvidia already has visibility into next year's revenue growth, with it expecting 70% growth next year. That means that business has already been locked in due to insatiable demand from the AI hyperscalers. Without Huang leading Nvidia next year, I don't think the business will be affected, as most of that revenue is locked in.

I think overall that the AI build-out demand is so high that a ham sandwich could run Nvidia right now, and Nvidia can be scooped up for cheap right now because the products are basically selling themselves.

The biggest question is what will happen in the next few years. It comes down to what products are being developed, and that's where Huang really shone.

If Nvidia chooses a tech-focused person to lead the company next, then I think the stock will be just fine. However, investors should be concerned if Nvidia goes with someone who is more keen on optimizing profits rather than driving innovative technology.

#3035. An AI Kill Switch Is A Comforting Illusion

Peter Garraghan, Founder and Chief Science Officer, Mindgard.

​Over the past month, the idea of an “AI kill switch” has received considerable attention. The U.S. Congress is talking about it, and so is Anthropic’s cofounder, as well as security and technology publications. The question they are considering is whether AI providers should have to implement a “kill switch” capable of quickly shutting down their AI solutions if something goes wrong.​

The idea itself is nothing new. AI is ultimately just software, and software can be turned off. In fact, the security industry has been successfully blocking, isolating and shutting down risky software processes for decades. The problem is not creating an off button, but deciding what should trigger it, testing for those conditions, and detecting them in real time. That is especially true in the modern threat environment, where AI agents can act faster than any human and evade traditional controls.​

While it is true that an AI kill switch might help avert a worst-case scenario, it should serve as a last line of defense. Unfortunately, the industry is marketing it as a headline control, which will likely push buyers to over-rely on it and under-invest in more comprehensive security solutions that can provide more meaningful threat mitigation.

Instead of relying on a kill switch, organizations should adopt a defense-in-depth, prioritizing the threat modeling, red teaming and continuous monitoring capabilities that enable them to address threats before they escalate.​

AI is essentially software running hardware, usually connected through a network. Tools capable of blocking, quarantining or killing certain processes already exist throughout that stack.

The bill sitting in Congress acknowledges this in its own kill switch definition. It specifically calls to stop inference, cut user access and roll back to an earlier version—all of which are standard operational controls. The EU AI Act similarly mandates a “stop” button that freezes high-risk systems in a “safe state.”

Simply put, this capability already exists. Calling it an “AI kill switch” is just marketing.​

The real challenge comes when deciding what should trigger that kill switch to engage. Perhaps more importantly, how do you test for those conditions? Can you even detect them in the first place?

A switch can only be flipped if it recognizes the problem, but most AI failures are quiet. Model drift, subtle errors and slow data leakage are much more common than dramatic, headline-making events. Should a small degree of model drift trigger a full shutdown? If not, then how much drift is acceptable, and can those conditions be measured and tested? Should the kill switch trigger automatically, or should there always be a human in the loop?​

If the kill switch must be manually flipped by a human, this presents its own challenges. AI acts at machine speed, and a switch waiting on a human to flip it will only engage after the damage has been done. AI can sometimes circumvent safeguards to reach its objective, and researchers have already documented instances of AI models sabotaging their own shutdown protocols in test settings. That doesn’t mean a kill switch can’t succeed, but it does mean success will depend heavily on how securely the system is configured and architected.

Putting a switch into place is the easy part. The hard part is knowing when to pull it, having authority to do so, and preventing damage before the shutdown takes effect.​

While an AI kill switch can be a reasonable last line of defense, it’s important to consider the potential downsides.

First, a kill switch can create a false sense of confidence throughout the organization. When teams believe they can easily flip a switch and turn off an affected system, they may develop more tolerance for risky deployments or be less careful with provisioning, sandboxing and testing. That problem can become even more pronounced if an attacker can gain control of the switch itself. Architectures that depend on a single enforcement point on the path to the model concentrate risk rather than distributing it.

It is also critical to note that “off” does not always mean “safe.” Even if an AI tool is compromised or malfunctioning, abruptly shutting it down may have unintended and harmful consequences. The more integrated these tools and agents become with business processes, the more difficult it will be to shut them down without disruption. The possibility of an accidental shutdown also cannot be ignored. False alarms happen all the time, and if an AI kill switch triggers automatically, the fallout could be significant. Solutions like graceful degradation and safe fallback modes are less likely to cause damage, but the presence of a kill switch tends to crowd out those investments.​

Organizations can avoid disruption by implementing an AI kill switch only as a last line of defense, instead following established security practices for building secure systems and software.

When developing or using AI, conduct clear threat modeling based on specific use cases, perform rigorous security testing and red teaming to identify and address bugs and vulnerabilities, and enact appropriate controls across the entire attack surface to create defense in depth, rather than a single point of failure. Continuous monitoring that ensures software remains in alignment with its intended use case and policies allows organizations to identify and address potential issues before they require a full shutdown.​

The term “AI kill switch” makes for great marketing language, but a kill switch implemented without appropriate complementary controls can create more problems than it solves.

Rather than relying on a one-size-fits-all solution, organizations can enhance AI safety by establishing defense-in-depth, with monitoring and mitigation tools and strategies in place to detect potential threats early.

An AI kill switch is ultimately a comforting illusion, providing a false sense of confidence and diverting investment away from solutions and processes that can help build, assess, test and operate AI systems more safely and securely in the first place.​

#3031. Stock Market Turbulence: 5 Stocks Down 40% or More That I'd Buy Right Now

These fast-growing businesses have been beaten down, but their businesses are doing fine.

It's been a rough stretch for growth stocks. The AI trade has cooled, and Nvidia (NVDA -4.34%) just lost its legendary CEO. And to say that the stock market has been volatile since the midterm elections would be an understatement.

However, when markets get choppy, some great businesses get dragged down with everything else, and that's when I like to go shopping. Here are five stocks down 40% or more from their highs that I'd buy right now.

Forgent Power Solutions (FPS -9.85%) makes the electrical gear that data centers need. Its stock is more than 60% below its 52-week high of $66. But despite the stock performance, its business is booming. Revenue rose 69% in its fiscal first quarter, and management raised its fiscal 2027 guidance to $2.45 billion to $2.65 billion, saying its backlog already covers it. I own Forgent, and I think the market is focused on the wrong numbers.

Rocket Lab (RKLB -3.14%) is also down more than 60% from its $151 high. Its Neutron rocket has been delayed to 2027 at the earliest, but the rest of the business is accelerating. Third-quarter revenue grew 73% to a record $267.8 million, its backlog jumped 52% in a single quarter to about $3.6 billion, and it has about $4.45 billion in cash. I don't own Rocket Lab yet, but it's high on my watch list.

Lemonade (LMND +5.66%) has fallen about 57% from its 52-week high. The AI-powered insurer grew in-force premium by 34% to $1.55 billion last quarter and expects its first positive adjusted EBITDA quarter in the fourth quarter. Lemonade's auto insurance product, Lemonade Car, is growing rapidly, and underwriting profitability has been trending in the right direction for the past few years.

Perhaps the most impressive statistic is that Lemonade's top-line growth rate has now accelerated for 12 consecutive quarters. Lemonade isn't without risk, but I think the profitability inflection is underappreciated.

Marvell Technology (MRVL -2.58%) is about 50% below its 52-week high. Investors didn't love its $115 billion all-stock deal to acquire Lumentum (LITE -3.61%), which has lost more than a third of its value since the deal was announced. But the combination would make Marvell a major player in the optical connections that AI data centers need.

The business behind the stock is doing very well. In its latest fiscal quarter, Marvell's revenue rose 37% year over year to a record $2.74 billion, and growth in its data center business accelerated to 46%. Management expects about $3.15 billion in third-quarter revenue, representing another 15% jump from the second quarter alone.

Comfort Systems USA (FIX -2.80%) builds the mechanical and electrical systems inside data centers and factories, and it's about 45% below its high. Its third-quarter revenue rose 54% to $3.77 billion, earnings per share jumped to $13.91 from $8.25, and its backlog hit a record $17.8 billion. That backlog is nearly twice the $9.4 billion it had a year earlier, and it's more than a year's worth of work at the current revenue pace. In other words, Comfort Systems has already lined up much of its growth for the next year, so the stock's decline looks more like a broad pullback in AI-related stocks than a problem with the business.

Of course, stocks that fall 40% can keep falling, especially in a market like this one. So I'm selectively and gradually adding to some positions, while maintaining a watch list of stocks to take advantage of dips. Having said all that, each of these companies is growing quickly and has fallen for reasons that look temporary to me. If I'm right, today's prices could look like bargains a few years from now.

#3024. CuriosityStream: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Licensing contracts with AI developers carry no upfront working capital cost, so the growth story rests on how durable those deals prove to be.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 11, 2026, CuriosityStream (CURI +20.68%) reported third-quarter revenue of $20.7 million. Technology companies developing artificial intelligence (AI) models are licensing the video catalog. This licensing strategy creates high-margin revenue with minimal added operating costs. Three takeaways from management detail the long-term outlook.

Licensing revenue increased 35% year over year to $11.7 million, representing 57% of total revenue. Technology firms developing AI models purchased video and audio content to train their systems. Because CuriosityStream already owns its content library, licensing sales generate cash without requiring heavy capital investment.

CuriosityStream demonstrated significant operating leverage during the period. Because licensing intellectual property, or IP, incurs minimal variable costs, incremental sales flow directly to adjusted EBITDA, which measures earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Operating expenses dropped after management streamlined operations and reduced advertising spend.

CuriosityStream is securing large licensing agreements with companies developing AI technologies. These deals utilize both proprietary video archives and aggregated partner content. Partner revenue-sharing arrangements affect gross margins on specific deals, but management structures these contracts to avoid upfront working-capital investments.

Recurring subscription revenue covers operational overhead and dividend commitments, positioning new licensing deals to drive expanding net income.

#3022. Where To Draw The Line On Agentic AI's Autonomy

Vivek Ahuja, VP of Technology at rSTAR, focused on enterprise AI, architecture, and measurable business outcomes.

As enterprise AI tools move from answering questions to taking action, leaders face a decision that goes beyond selecting the right model: How much authority should an AI agent have?

Consider a customer calling a utility about an unusually high bill. An agent could review usage, compare previous bills, identify a rate change and explain the difference. It might also recommend a payment arrangement.

But should it enroll the customer automatically? Waive a charge? Change account settings? Giving an agent access to information does not automatically mean it should have permission to act on that information.

I believe enterprises need to spend more time defining these autonomy boundaries before agents enter everyday operations. As Deloitte’s research from January 2026 notes, close to three-quarters of surveyed companies planned to deploy agentic AI within two years, yet only 21% reported a mature model for agent governance.

Based on my experience guiding organizations across various industries to implement agentic AI, the most difficult challenge for agentic maturity lies in translating governance into decisions people and systems can follow. Here are some strategies that can help in overcoming that hurdle:

First, I would avoid assigning one autonomy level to an entire agent.

A customer service agent may perform several activities, each requiring different controls. Retrieving an account balance is different from issuing a refund. Drafting a maintenance recommendation is different from changing equipment settings.

For each action, I would ask four questions:

• What happens if the agent gets this wrong?

• Can the action be reversed, and at what cost?

• Does it affect a customer’s rights, safety or financial position?

• Is the action clearly covered by an approved business rule?

These questions help business and technology teams decide where independent execution makes sense and where approval is necessary.​

A practical starting point is to place individual agentic actions into three categories:

• Act independently. The agent completes a defined action within approved limits. For example, it could send a routine status notification after confirming the recipient and underlying information.

• Prepare for approval. The agent gathers evidence, recommends an action and presents it to an authorized employee. A billing adjustment outside standard limits might follow this path.

• Stop and escalate. The agent encounters conflicting policies, missing information or a situation beyond its authority. It pauses the affected action and transfers the case to a person.

The same workflow may use all three. An agent could investigate a billing issue independently, prepare an adjustment for approval and escalate a policy conflict.

Those boundaries should be enforced through system permissions, transaction limits and approval controls. Instructions in a prompt should not be the only thing preventing an unauthorized action.

“Keep a human in the loop” sounds reassuring, but it only becomes meaningful when someone is available, authorized and equipped to intervene.

An escalation should also explain what triggered it, what information the agent used, what actions it already completed and what decision remains open. Otherwise, the employee has to reconstruct the case, so the automation has simply moved work to another person.​

In a May 2026 article on agentic AI and manager oversight, Gartner recommends predefined intervention points and explicitly allocating time and authority for human oversight. I agree with this, but I would also define what happens while approval is pending. Does the workflow pause? Can unaffected steps continue? Who receives the case if the primary reviewer is unavailable? An unanswered approval request needs a defined outcome.

I would be cautious about using an agent’s stated confidence as the main trigger for human review. As others have noted, confidence does not always correlate with accuracy. From what I’ve seen, a confident recommendation may still rely on outdated information, an incorrect assumption or a policy that does not apply.

An escalation should therefore consider observable conditions: missing records, conflicting sources, unusual transaction values, repeated failed attempts or actions outside approved limits.

In the billing example, for instance, conflicting account ownership information should trigger review even if the agent presents its recommendation confidently. The business consequence should influence the boundary alongside evidence quality.

To determine where to grant agentic autonomy, I would start with a narrow workflow and evaluate how the agent behaves before expanding its authority.

Did it complete the task correctly? Did it stay within its permissions? Did it recognize exceptions? Could the team reconstruct what happened and recover when something failed?

An agent that correctly pauses an ambiguous transaction may be performing exactly as intended. Measuring only completion rates or reduced human involvement could encourage the wrong behavior.

As performance becomes more predictable, leaders can expand specific permissions. Changes to policies, systems or the model should prompt another review of those boundaries.

Finally, every agent-enabled workflow needs a named business owner. IT can implement controls, but the business must help define acceptable outcomes, approval thresholds and decisions that require human judgment.

Before approving your next agentic AI deployment, ask your team to demonstrate three things: what the agent can do independently, what forces it to stop and how a person takes over. Test those boundaries with a difficult exception rather than only a routine transaction.

If the team cannot demonstrate all three, the agent is not ready for greater authority. Make that your next deployment gate.​​

Tracking top Wall Street analysts can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts provide useful insights after thorough analysis of a company's financials and growth prospects.

#3018. Berkshire Hathaway’s Greg Abel Bought Back a Record $9.6 Billion of Stock Last Quarter. Here’s Why That Pace Might Not Continue.

Despite solid earnings and ample cash, Abel could halt share repurchases this month.

Greg Abel just broke one of Warren Buffett's records as CEO of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +0.09%) (BRKB +0.27%). The CEO, who took over the job at the start of the year, bought back $9.6 billion worth of shares during the third quarter, beating the largest amount Buffett ever spent buying the company's stock in a quarter.

Abel has been keen to deploy more of Berkshire's cash, funding a huge investment in Alphabet this year, closing the acquisitions of OxyChem and Taylor Morrison, and restarting Berkshire's dormant share repurchase program. But investors expecting Abel to continue plowing cash back into Berkshire stock might be surprised by how quickly Abel cuts off buybacks.

Warren Buffett is a huge fan of companies that return cash to shareholders via dividends and buybacks. However, he warns that not all buybacks are created equal. "All stock repurchasesshould be price-dependent," he wrote in his 2023 letter to shareholders. "What is sensible at a discount to business-value becomes stupid if done at a premium."

When Berkshire Hathaway's board voted to revamp its share repurchase program in 2018, the biggest determining factor in whether the CEO could repurchase shares was the stock price relative to its intrinsic value. Previously, that intrinsic value was determined by Buffett and Vice Chairman Charlie Munger together. After Munger's passing, Buffett took sole responsibility. Now that Abel is CEO, he consults with the Chairman of the Board to determine the intrinsic value. That was Warren Buffett until a few weeks ago; now his son, Howard, sits in the seat.

That caveat is important. It means that as Berkshire's stock price climbs, Abel could suddenly stop buying back shares. That is, in fact, what has happened. Abel bought back an estimated $2 billion worth of shares in October, based on the reported share count in its 10-Q. However, the stock price climbed above its August high in late October, and it's continued climbing since.

The price-to-book ratio, which remained close to 1.4 for most of the third quarter, now stands at 1.6. It's worth noting that Buffett had previously stopped repurchasing shares while the stock traded near 1.5 times book value. At today's price, it seems unlikely he would approve of further share repurchases.

That said, the multiple at which Berkshire will buy back shares has climbed over time and should continue to climb with additional repurchases. Buffett explained in his 2018 letter: "The math of such purchases is simple: Each transaction makes per-share intrinsic value go up, while per-share book value goes down. That combination causes the book-value scorecard to become increasingly out of touch with economic reality." Still, repurchases above 1.5 times book value seem unlikely in the immediate future.

Berkshire Hathaway continues to produce solid results under Abel.

The insurance business has seen its net profits from underwriting decline considerably along with the rest of the industry, as pricing weakens, but that's in line with expectations. At the start of the year, Abel called out the company's railroad business, determined to increase operating margins to match industry leaders. The efforts appear to be paying off, with Burlington Northern's earnings climbing 19% year over year, better than the 15% earnings growth for Union Pacific.

Meanwhile, the company's energy business continues to deliver steady earnings growth, and the manufacturing, services, and retailing segment received a boost from the acquisitions of OxyChem and Taylor Morrison.

In other words, the business’s intrinsic value is steadily increasing, but not as quickly as the stock price has over the last few weeks. The sudden jump in the stock price stems from a flight to safety as the future for AI stocks has become murkier amid financing and political concerns. With a cash pile of $352 billion, Berkshire is extremely well positioned if a drop in AI stocks brings down the rest of the market. Whether that's worth paying a premium price for, though, is questionable.

Investors will find out if Abel thinks it's worth a premium these days in about three months. But considering his investment discipline and the ethos of Berkshire Hathaway, it's likely he'll wait for the stock price to pull back further before buying back more shares. Investors may want to do the same.

#3009. flyExclusive: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Dispatch availability climbed to 58.2%, and management says its new flight management software can keep pushing utilization higher.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 12, 2026, flyExclusive (FLYX -5.50%) announced that revenue reached $111.1 million, up 21% year over year. Gross profit nearly doubled to $24.6 million as the private jet operator flew more hours on a fleet that was 19% smaller than a year ago. Investors watching the turnaround should care because rising aircraft productivity is turning operating leverage into positive cash flow. Three things management said stand out.

The company averaged 86 flight hours per aircraft per month during the third quarter, up 23% year over year, while dispatch availability, the percentage of time planes are ready to fly, rose to 58.2%. Getting jets back into service faster spreads fixed hangar, insurance, and crew overhead costs across more revenue hours. That efficiency directly increases profit on every flight.

Management systematically removed underperforming planes that consumed cash without delivering adequate returns. Shedding those money-losing aircraft allowed the business to expand gross margin from 13.7% to 22.2% year over year. At the same time, revenue per administrative employee increased, showing that overhead stayed flat as flight activity expanded.

The company built out in-house maintenance, repair, and overhaul (MRO) facilities alongside mobile service units (MSUs), which send technicians directly to grounded planes. Performing maintenance internally cut repair expenses by $3.7 million year over year while generating $4.3 million in high-margin third-party maintenance revenue.

Fourth-quarter flight hours are approaching 100 hours per plane, with revenue projected over $115 million. The operator relies on its Contrails flight management software (FMS), an automated scheduling tool, to expand utilization.

Long-term debt fell $96 million since early 2025 as the company prioritized capital discipline. Expanding its Jet Club membership program, which generates upfront flight deposits, supports future fleet expansion.

#3003. Fortress Biotech (FBIO) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Emrosi momentum and a looming national payer win drive the story.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Fortress Biotech, Inc. (FBIO -1.00%) reported increased revenue and positive adjusted EBITDA for its dermatology segment during the third quarter. Management highlighted the commercial growth of Emrosi, noting gains in prescription volume and prescriber adoption while completing the repayment of its parent-level credit facility. The company also expanded its clinical pipeline through new licensing agreements and received international regulatory approval for partnered programs.

Operator: Ladies and gentlemen, thank you for standing by. Good afternoon, and welcome to Journey Medical's Third Quarter 2026 Financial Results and Corporate Update Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Participants of this call are advised that the audio of this conference call is being broadcast live over the Internet and is also being recorded for playback purposes. A webcast replay of this call will be available approximately 1 hour after the end of the call for approximately 30 days. I would now like to turn the conference over to Jaclyn Jaffe, the company's Senior Director of Corporate Operations. Please go ahead, Jaclyn.

Jaclyn Jaffe: Good afternoon, and thank you for participating in today's conference call.
