#2903. Why was Jordan Davis listening to Sinatra on his way out of Philly?

Why was Jordan Davis listening to Sinatra on his way out of Philly? originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The trade came as a shock to Jordan Davis, so on the way to the airport he did the only thing that made sense.

"On the way to the airport in Philly, I had a moment," Davis said. "You know, I try not to cry. But I prayed and I played a song, Frank Sinatra, My Way.And I think that just encompasses (my) time there and just stepping away and looking toward a new future.

"And obviously the time that I spent in Philly, I have grown so much from that. And I became a better player because of it, became a better man because of it. And it's not to say that I would ever sit there and turn my back on Philly. I don't have any hate. I don't have any bad blood against it. It's just the business of it all. It's just the nature of the beast. And I'm grateful for the time. But right now, I have another mission. I have a new city to embrace.

"Excited to give back to the community and get into the community and see what all Jacksonville has to offer. So I guess I'll save that for retirement. When I really do the big big cry."

Davis spoke to Jacksonville media on Wednesday and clips from that interview were posted on social media by Action Sports Jacksonville, which is Jacksonville's CBS47 and Fox30.

The Eagles traded Davis, their 2022 1st-round pick, to the Jaguars Monday for a 1st-round pick in 2027 and a 2nd-round pick in 2029.

"Excited to work," Davis said. "We have a good front, so excited to be a piece of it. I'm just optimistic about the future. I'm optimistic about me playing a role and being who I am and being a piece to add to this already good team. And however far that we go, I hope to be a part of it."

Davis was a four-year starter with the Eagles, a starter on two Super Bowl teams, a champion in 2024 and a key figure on the NFL's No.1 defense. He had a Pro Bowl-caliber season in 2025 but his play dropped off in the nine games he played for the Eagles this year before the trade.

Jaguars general manager James Gladstone said in a Zoom call with Jaguars media he believes defensive tackles in general can continue to improve well into their career and thinks Davis still has plenty of up-side."That was a major piece to the puzzle," he said. "It's a position that ages well, and certainly look forward to seeing him integrate here in the early going, while at the same time knowing that it's going to be a longer-term decision that we made with him.

"And so making sure that his development over these next few years is something we really think through and plan out. But I think that's certainly a big piece to the why behind it."

With the trade, Davis went from last place to first place, from two wins to six wins. And he went from the NFL's No. 19 scoring defense to No. 2.

And if the Jags' defense seems a little familiar it's because defensive coordinator Anthony Campanile was Vic Fangio's linebackers coach with the Dolphins in 2023 and runs a lot of similar stuff.

"I'm here to do whatever the coaches need me to do," Davis said. "Still figuring out a plan, still learning the playbook. … It's easy to learn. It's different languages. The structure is the same as coming from Philly. The defensive structure is the same. It's the same tree. But it's just learning the language, learning the little nuances of the play.

"It's a lot more fronts that they play here than they did in Philly with a little bit of effort (on the) grindstone, and I'll be here a little bit later (Wednesday) night, I'll pick it up soon."

Jaguars coach Liam Coen recalled coaching against Davis in college and just being struck by his size.

"Jordan Davis, competed against and coached against him when he was at Georgia in 2021 when I was at Kentucky," he said. "And I just remember him walking the field pregame and I'm wondering how we're gonna gain a yard with that defense they had there.

"A young ascending player that has a lot of freakish traits. I mean, he's a large human being that can move, that does have some rush and some three-down value."

Davis joins a terrific defensive line that also includes Georgia teammate Travon Walker, the first pick in the 2022 draft that also produced Davis at No. 13 overall.

"It's gonna be great having my brother back with me," Walker said in another Action Sports video. "If you look back at the season he had last year and the season that he's going to put together this year, obviously, he's still potentially is going to grow every day and that's something that we always preach on there, at Georgia and I'm pretty sure his coaches (with the Eagles) preached on that really as well and now, he's here. So now coach is pretty saying the same thing every day. He wants to get better every day."

The Jaguars on Monday also acquired safety Jessie Bates from the Falcons and defensive tackle Davon Godchaux from the Saints as they reshaped their defense for the stretch run.

"I'm a big guy too, but Jordan, he's huge," Godchaux said. "I'm excited to play with him, feed off him, him feed off me. It's gonna be special. We've got a great group of guys who were already here before we got here. So we're all gonna come together. But, I mean, we have a great group on paper. We still gotta go out and prove it each and every week."

Davis leaves a team that's lost seven straight games and joins a team that on Sunday will be going for its sixth win in seven games. The Jags face the Titans in Nashville Sunday afternoon. The Jags already had their bye so Davis could play 18 games this year.

"I think anybody who plays in big games has a certain level of experience that they could bring to another level," Davis said. "It takes a lot to go to the Super Bowl and it takes a lot to win a Super Bowl, but. right now we're in the process of just one game at a time.

"I know everybody likes to look to the end goal, but you can't see at the end if you don't see where your feet are right now. So I'm just enjoying it, enjoying the moment, looking for my first dub as a Jag, hopefully this weekend. We'll figure out where it goes at the end."

#2901. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.â¯â¯

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2899. Winners, losers as Commanders upset Giants 24-21 on Thursday Night Football

Winners, losers as Commanders upset Giants 24-21 on Thursday Night Football originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Washington Commanders are still alive.

Washington kept its playoff chances somewhat intact with a 24-21 road victory at the New York Giants on Thursday Night Football.

Jayden Daniels needed to outduel J.J. McCarthy to give his team a shot. Despite throwing two poorly timed interceptions, it was a McCarthy error that sealed the game.

Washington is 3-5-1 with some hope in the NFC East, while the Giants are still on top at 5-3-1. This could be a loss they look back on as a missed opportunity, though.

Let's break down the Week 10 opener further with winners and losers:

Besides tight end Isaiah Likely's strong game, Washington's Diggs was the best receiver on the night. Diggs hauled in six catches on 10 targets for 83 yards and a score.

He most notably delivered the catch-and-run touchdown late in the fourth to give Washington the lead. And it came with a memorable celebration. Diggs did his talking and backed it up.

It wasn't a bad game by McCarthy, but he will rue some of the missed opportunities throughout. New York should've put the game away sooner after Daniels' initial two interceptions. Instead, it came down to a McCarthy heave that resulted in Rasul Douglas sealing the deal.

McCarthy threw for 293 yards, one touchdown and one pick while completing 23 of 36 passes. The offense needs a bit more juice to stay atop the division.

Every passing week it gets clearer the Commanders have a defensive cornerstone in rookie linebacker Styles.

He was tied with Giants rising star Arvell Reese in total tackles with 13, though he had more solos with five and one tackle for loss. McCarthy had a stiff arm on him earlier in the game, but he compensated with a key third-down tackle to continue adding to his first-year tape.

New York needs Nabers to take that leap at some point. He had four straight strong 100-yard performances earlier in the season, but the last two weeks against divisional opponents have brought him down to earth.

Consistent quarterback play and serious injuries have hindered his development, but he lost a fumble in this one and could've done much better on the 50/50 ball Douglas picked off. He finished the game with five catches on seven targets for 44 yards, though one 30-plus-yard play did the heavy lifting.

The NFC East doesn't have a clear favorite. The Philadelphia Eagles won't be in the conversation without Jalen Hurts, while the Dallas Cowboys lack the defense and consistency. That leaves New York and Washington.

New York's hopes relies on McCarthy doing just enough and Washington also slipping up in its pursuit to the top. Both teams have several tough opponents coming the rest of the way, so it's difficult to predict the division winner. The Giants have the record edge, but Washington secured a key result to keep the pressure on. A loss would've all but ended the season, but the Commanders now own the tiebreaker in this matchup.

#2897. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2894. 25 YEARS SINCE CARLO'S HOMECOMING

A homecoming that took place 25 years ago. On November 13th, 2001, Carlo Ancelotti took charge of AC Milan for the first time, having previously played for the Club from 1987 to 1992. It marked the beginning of an eight-year spell filled with trophies, including the Scudetto, Club World Cup and two Champions League titles. It was a landmark moment in the Club's modern history, preceded by a decisive few days. On November 4th, the Rossoneri suffered a 1-0 league defeat away to Torino. The following day, Club officials, led by Adriano Galliani, drove to Parma. Ancelotti had already reached a verbal agreement with the Emilia-Romagna club and only needed to sign the contract, but the pull of AC Milan was simply too strong to resist. The Rossoneri secured his appointment, and Ancelotti was officially unveiled on November 6th, 2001.

Twenty-five years ago, the current Brazil head coach returned home to replace the dismissed Fatih Terim following an inconsistent start to the season. For Ancelotti, it was an emotional homecoming, first at Milanello, where he received a touching welcome from Silvano Ramaccioni, and then at San Siro. He had already enjoyed an extraordinary spell there as a player, winning European Cups, league titles and international trophies with Arrigo Sacchi's "Immortals" and Fabio Capello's "Invincibles", before ending his playing career with the Rossoneri in 1992. That 3-0 victory over Perugia marked the beginning of a legendary eight-season era, during which AC Milan won one Scudetto, one Coppa Italia, one Italian Super Cup, two Champions League titles, two UEFA Super Cups and the Club World Cup in Yokohama against Boca Juniors, in a true clash of world football giants.

#2891. Eagles continue reshaping roster by reportedly releasing veteran wide receiver

Eagles continue reshaping roster by reportedly releasing veteran wide receiver originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Hollywood Brown's brief and odd Eagles career is over after four games and five catches.

The Eagles are releasing Brown by mutual consent of the player and the team, Jordan Schultz of the Scultz Report posted on social media Thursday night.

The Eagles signed Brown to a one-year, $5 million contract in March with the expectation that he'd be a significant part of the Eagles' passing game following the departure of A.J. Brown. But even with Brown gone and DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks both hurt much of the season, Brown has been an afterthought.

He's only played in four of the Eagles' nine games, getting nine targets and catching five passes for 63 yards along with two rushing attempts for 12 yards. His longest catch was a 19-yarder from Tanner McKee in the loss to the Cowboys. He had a season-high 30 yards in that game.

Brown expressed frustration with his limited role in a recent conversation with NBC Sports Philadelphia:

"Of course, you want to be somewhere where you play, but at the ultimate end of the day, it's not my plan. So I might want to play, I might want to contribute, but if that's not the plan for me, I can't dwell on it too much or you'll be unhappy. And then you're taking that back home. I can't take that back home to the wife and kids. So I gotta try to stay as positive as I can."

Asked how that was going, he laughed and said, "Some days better than others."

He added: "We're all dealing with something and we're dealing with something as a team. So I don't wanna add that extra stress."

Brown said he expected to play more but was dealing with it because he didn't want to put personal issues ahead of team needs.

"I did (expect a bigger role), but I do understand stuff happens," he said. "I mean, you can't take anything personal in this business, so you kind of just got to go about your process and when that time happens, whatever happens, you've just got to be ready for it."

Brown, a 1st-round pick in 2019, had a 1,000-yard season for the Ravens in 2021 and over 700 yards in both 2020 and 2022. He was 49-for-587 with five touchdowns last year. In his career, he has 378 catches for 4,385 yards and 33 touchdowns.

The Eagles, 2-7, have lost seven straight game. Their offense is ranked 32nd in the NFL in yards per game, 30th in points per game and 29th in passing yards per game.

With Brown no longer on the roster, the Eagles have rookie 1st-round pick Makai Lemon, 2nd-year undrafted free agent Darius Cooper and Elijah Moore as healthy wide receivers. Smith and Wicks have both missed the last two games but could be back after the bye week.

Lemon is the Eagles' leading receiver with 37-for-312 and a touchdown and Smith (26-310-2), Wicks (19-284-1) and Cooper (13-200-4) are next.

The Eagles have two WRs on the practice squad – Erik Ezukanma and Xavier Gipson.

Because it's after the trade deadline, Brown is subject to the waiver claim process despite being a vested veteran with seven years of pension credit.

#2886. Warning to NFL general managers: Howie Roseman is about to fleece you

Warning to NFL general managers: Howie Roseman is about to fleece you originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

A broken-down receiver, a washed-up guard and an underachieving defensive tackle walk into a bar. And Howie Roseman walks out with two 1st-round picks and three 2nd-round picks.

If I were an NFL general manager, I wouldn't even answer the phone when Howie calls. Because you're going to get fleeced.

And if there's one thing to feel good about during this miserable season to nowhere, it's the way Howie has stockpiled premium draft picks with a series of what sure seem like lopsided trades.

It all started in June when Roseman shipped disgruntled and divisive A.J. Brown to the Patriots for a 1st-round pick in 2027 and a 5th-round pick in 2027.

Brown was one of the best in the league not too long ago. He owns the two-best receiving seasons in Eagles history – 88-for-1,496 and 11 TDs in 2022 and 106-for-1,456-7 in 2023.

But it sure seems like his knees won't allow him to be that kind of player again. The A.J. Brown we saw hobbling around and dropping passes in his final game in an Eagles uniform, the playoff loss to the 49ers in January, seems to be closer to the player he is now than the Pro Bowler of a few years ago.

The Patriots got a WR whose balky knees have allowed him to play only three games so far this year, with just nine catches for 101 yards, three drops and a fumble.

Of 95 WRs who've been targetted at least 20 times so far this year, Brown's 15 percent drop percentage is highest. And his catch percentage is 89th. Maybe he gets healthy the second half of the season and reverts back to that three-time Pro Bowler. Or maybe this is just who A.J. Brown is right now. Either way the Eagles got a 1st-round pick for a guy who had no future in Philadelphia, didn't want to be here and may be a shot fighter at 29.

Then came the Cam Jurgens trade, and it's still hard to believe the Ravens donated the Eagles a 2nd-round pick for a guy who just hasn't been the same player as he was in 2024. Jurgens was a tremendous interior lineman before his career was derailed by injuries. Although he made a second straight Pro Bowl last year, he was only a shell of the player he was in 2023 and 2024. This year, he struggled both at center and guard before Howie someone finagled a 2nd-round pick out of the Ravens for him.

How has it gone in Baltimore for Jurgens? You always take Pro Football Focus grades with a grain of salt, but out of 89 interior linemen who've played at least 300 snaps his 43.9 combined grade with the Eagles and Ravens is 4th-worst.

In Jurgens' three games in Baltimore, the Ravens – with both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley hurt – have scored 33 points, gone 0-3, losing 35-13 to the Bengals, 36-10 to the Bills and 33-10 to the Jaguars after starting the season 4-1.

Then there's Jordan Davis. He was a good player here and at times a very good player. He probably should have made the Pro Bowl last year. But overall? As the 13th pick in the 2022 draft and the 10th-highest paid interior lineman in the league? He's underachieved, in particular this year. He was a key starter on two Super Bowl teams but he just never dominated the way you wanted him to, the way he's being paid to. And he played just 52 percent of the snaps in his nine games this year, which ranks 27th among starting interior linemen so far this year.

Great guy, natural leader, inspiring backstory, solid lineman. But hardly indispensable. Not when you also have Jalen Carter under contract and Moro Ojomo now in line for an offseason extension. The Eagles don't really have a third high-level rotational defensive tackle now – Ty Robinson, a 4th-round pick last year, will likely take over the role. But if you can get 1st- and 2nd-round draft picks for essentially a part-time player that's a no-brainer.

Add it up and Roseman got 1st-round picks in 2027 and 2028, 2nd-round picks in 2027, 2028 and 2029 and a couple 2027 5th-round picks to boot for a beat-up wide receiver, a fading interior lineman and a defensive tackle who hasn't lived up to expectations.

The way Roseman drafts, most of those picks are going to turn into important pieces as the Eagles build back toward respectability.

There hasn't been a whole lot to celebrate on the field this year, and everything points to more of the same over these last eight games.

But up on the second floor of the Jefferson Health Training Complex, Roseman is doing all he can to make sure it doesn't happen again.

#2884. Jordan Davis trade grades: Eagles celebrated, Jaguars dragged

Jordan Davis trade grades: Eagles celebrated, Jaguars dragged originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The widely expected Eagles sell-off began Monday afternoon, when they shipped defensive tackle Jordan Davis to the Jacksonville Jaguars for a first-round draft pick in the 2027 draft, as well as a second-round pick in the 2029 draft. Considering Davis' underwhelming play this season and his exorbitant price tag, the return Eagles executive VP/GM Howie Roseman got for Davis was impressive.

But what did those who cover the NFL at large think about the deal? We culled opinions from several sites to find out.

Daniel Popper: Senior NFL Writer, The Athletic – Eagles A+, Jaguars D-

"This was a significant overpay for general manager James Gladstone and the Jaguars. Davis is a very good run defender, but he does not offer nearly enough as a pass rusher to command this type of return."

"This is an impressive return for Roseman, and the Eagles are now loaded with future draft capital. They now have two first-round picks in 2027, and two first-round picks in 2028, their own and the Patriots' pick from the A.J. Brown trade. They also own two second-round picks in 2029. That is a ton of ammunition to use in whatever fashion Roseman sees fit. They could stick and pick. They could trade any of those picks for veteran players. They could package them to move around in the draft. As our Jonathan Jones wrote in our trade deadline live blog, "What the Eagles have now is total optionality."

"Roseman is doing the right thing, turning a losing season into future opportunity."

Garrett Podell: NFL Writer, CBS Sports – Eagles A, Jaguars B-

"Jacksonville's timing is right, but the price they paid for Davis is not. A first-round pick and a second-round pick for Davis isn't all that different from what the Chicago Bears gave up (2027 and 2028 first-round picks plus a 2027 fourth-round pick) for All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons, the best defensive tackle in football at the moment. Sure, the Jaguars were never going to get Simmons since he was on an AFC South division rival, the Tennessee Titans, but couldn't they have gotten Davis for a slightly cheaper price?"

"Roseman… recouped first-round and second-round draft capital in the deal. He couldn't have asked for anything more, and he'll be able to rebuild the Eagles in no time with 10 picks in the first two rounds of the draft across the next three years."

Kevin Patra: NFL.com Senior News Writer – Eagles A, Jaguars C+

"The Jags' massive move for nose tackle Jordan Davis comes with huge risk for the GM who took the F-them picks mantle from his former boss, Les Snead, and sped away. If it works out and Davis plays a pivotal role in the Jags bringing a Lombardi to Duval, no one will give a hoot how much it costs. If it backfires, Davis struggles and Jacksonville bows out early, it will be a tough pill to swallow."

"I'm not sure Howie Roseman wanted to move Davis after just paying him, but when you get a desperate trade partner offering the moon, sometimes you just take their cash and hightail it out of there. This feels akin to the reaction when the Raiders initially got two first-round picks from the Ravens for Maxx Crosby before the trade was scuttled."

Seth Walder: NFL Analytics Writer, ESPN – Eagles A, Jaguars F

"There's no other way to start this: This trade is a disaster for Jacksonville.

"The Jaguars are absolutely right to go for it. Entering Monday, they had a 12.8% chance to win the Super Bowl, per ESPN's Football Power Index, second highest of any team and best in the AFC. And that's why I was OK with them rolling the dice on Jessie Bates III earlier in the day. A late-round pick for Davon Godchaux, too? Fine. But first- and second-round picks for Davis? Reckless would be putting it mildly."

"With Philadelphia's season effectively over, it's easy for the Eagles to unload their players. But at this kind of price, I'm inclined to think the Eagles would have dealt Davis no matter their contention status or depth chart. Philadelphia should be thrilled at this return."

#2883. Trump says ‘we don’t need a deal with Iran’

The U.S. should no longer seek a deal with Iran, President Donald Trump said Thursday as he directed Vice President JD Vance to instead focus his attention on corruption and crime in Democratic cities and states.

"We don't need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed," Trump wrote in a Truth Social post.

"We've got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we've got their Strait!" Trump wrote, referring to the Strait of Hormuz, a key choke point in the ongoing U.S.-Iran war.

The announcement came just over a week after Republicans suffered huge losses in the midterm election. Vance and other Republicans have pinned those losses on the Iran war, which the U.S. and Israel started in late February.

In the weeks leading up to Election Day, Trump repeatedly suggested the U.S. could resume launching military strikes, which have largely subsided as a tense stalemate has taken hold in the region.

The Wall Street Journal on Wednesday reported that Vance, who has led diplomatic efforts with Iran in prior months, was considering face-to-face talks with a top Iranian security official.

Stock futures declined following Trump's post.

The unpopular Iran war, which Trump and others in his administration said would be won in a matter of weeks, is now in its ninth month with no clear end in sight. The conflict has driven up global energy prices, creating a ripple effect through the economy and exacerbating concerns about inflation and affordability that were paramount to voters in the midterms.

Corruption emerged as another top issue that broke in Democrats' favor in the election. Trump's post Thursday morning attempts to flip the script, blaming Democratic-run areas for allowing corruption and crime to proliferate.

"I have asked Vice President Vance to focus his complete and total attention on the Massive Corruption and Crime taking place in Democrat run Cities and States throughout the U.S.," Trump wrote.

"The Blue States, such as Minnesota, California, Illinois, and many more, are all poorly run, corrupt disasters. Billions of Dollars is being stolen each and every month," the president wrote. "Our Budget could be quickly BALANCED with the correction of this large scale criminal activity."

"CORRUPTION is in the Democrat Leadership's blood!" he added.

#2882. Florida State basketball vs. Texas A&M-Corpus Christi: Preview, how to watch, game thread

Coming off its first loss of the season, Florida State men's hoops (2-1) face Texas A&M-Corpus Christi (2-1) in a "get-right" game after Tuesday's defeat to Florida.

In that rivalry matchup, the Seminoles hung tough against the #1 team in the country, but ultimately, UF's rebounding advantage (41-29) and FSU's inability to knock down threes (4-21) snuck the Seminoles. However, even though head coach Luke Loucks said he does not believe in moral victories, seeing the fight and physicality against the Gators inspires confidence in what FSU can do against worse competition.

Seminoles run out of gas against No. 1 Florida, lose 81-72

Speaking of a change in competition, for years, fans and media alike got upset with Leonard Hamilton for scheduling tricky Q3 or Q4 games instead of scheduling those at the bottom of the NET rankings. Loucks did not make the same mistakes, and TAMCC finished last season 190th in NET rankings while being picked to finish 3rd in the Southland conference's preseason poll.

Florida State game notes from FSU stats and info:

Florida State continues its six-game homestand on Friday night as the Seminoles host Texas A&M-Corpus Christi inside the Donald L. Tucker Center at 5 p.m. on ACC Network Extra. The game will be sponsored by VyStar Credit Union as part of Military Appreciation.

Florida State suffered its first loss of the season this past Tuesday, falling to rival and No. 1-ranked Florida by a final score of 81-72. While the Seminoles were able to outscore the Gators in the paint, 32-20, it was Florida who had the upperhand in rebounding, tallying 12 more than FSU. The Gators also made a living at the free throw line, sinking 30 of them at the charity stripe. The 41 free throw attempts by Florida tied for the most ever by an opponent in the Tucker Center (FIU, Dec. 12, 2004).

Despite taking a season-low 18 attempts at the foul line, the Seminoles continued to make the most of it by making 14. Through three games, Florida State is shooting 82.4 percent from the free throw line, which ranks 12th in the nation (75- of-91). Their average of 25.0 free throws made per game ranks seventh.

Sebastian Rancik scored a game-high 23 points in the loss to top-ranked Florida. After registering just six points in the first half, the junior forward poured in 17 in the second stanza. He is currently averaging a team-best 22.0 points per game, which ranks 30th in the country. He has totaled 66 points so far, whichis the most by a Seminole through the first three games of a season since 2015 (Malik Beasley).

Anthony Robinson II netted a season-high 19 points against Florida, going 6-of-12 from the field. The senior guard came into the contest with lots of experience playing the Gators from his time at Missouri. It marked the fifth time he has ever faced Florida. In those five games, he is averaging 13.4 points.

Kam Taylor reached double-digit points for the third time this season, registering 10 against the Gators. The junior guard is currently shooting 54.5 percent from the field, having made 12 of his 22 field goal attempts. Taylor and Rancik are the only Seminoles who have scored in double figures in all threegames this season.

Marcis Ponder was voted ACC Rookie of the Week for week one after averaging 12.5 points and 10.5 rebounds in the two victories over Florida A&M and Troy. The freshman center became the first Seminole since Cam Corhen in 2022 to earn ACC Rookie of the Week honors.

#2881. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2880. Why Singapore keeps turning up in the fight over Nvidia chips and China

Singapore, a hub for billions of dollars' worth of Nvidia sales each year, finds itself increasingly in the crosshairs of Washington's push to stop advanced AI chips from reaching China, as the chipmaker CEO's scandal once again thrusts the city-state into the spotlight.

The Southeast Asian nation said Thursday that it had barred Alice Huang, the businesswoman at the center of a scandal that catalyzed the end of Jensen Huang's run as Nvidia CEO, from re-entering the country.

A fifth person was handed 41 charges on Friday in a Singapore fraud case involving computer servers that might have contained Nvidia chips and were exported to Malaysia. The alleged transactions in the broader case totaled about $390 million, prosecutors said March last year.

The investigations into several companies and key officials, including Megaspeed International, began in February 2025 and remained ongoing as of Thursday.

Authorities have been examining whether servers exported through Singapore to Malaysia without entering the domestic market were subsequently rerouted to another destination, contrary to declarations made in Singapore, the Ministry of Home Affairs said in a statement late Thursday.

Singapore has faced growing international scrutiny, particularly from the U.S., over its role as a major billing and potential transit hub for Nvidia chips that U.S. officials say have reached China.

The U.S. has imposed export restrictions on Nvidia's most cutting-edge AI chips to China since 2022 to curb Beijing's surging AI development.

Nvidia's revenue linked to Singapore rose to 22% in the fiscal quarter ending July 2025, up from 8% in 2023, with the country's trade ministry estimating chips physically delivered there accounted for less than 1% of the chipmaker's revenue.

Experts have pointed to gaps in enforcement of U.S. export restrictions and Singapore's corporate system that make it hard to trace where advanced Nvidia chips ultimately end up.

Nvidia chips can be legally sold to customers in other countries, such as Malaysia or Singapore, and then illicitly resold or re-exported to China. Chinese companies can also legally gain remote access to the most advanced AI chips hosted in data centers outside China rather than buying the hardware directly.

Chris McGuire, a senior fellow at Council on Foreign Relations and former U.S. national security official, said that Southeast Asia has become "a major hub for chip smuggling and export control circumvention," importing tens of billions of dollars' worth of AI chips per year.

Singapore has stepped up efforts to police suspected chip diversion since last year, including investigations involving alleged fraud and money laundering.

Authorities acknowledged the potential "blind spots between separate monitoring of trade flows and banking flows," said Alvin Camba, lead scientist and director of research at Lyvi, an Austin-based AI risk assessment firm. Correlating physical shipments with related financial transactions can be "challenging," he added.

"Singapore's strong corporate secrecy regulations, which offer extensive protections for foreign investors, can also make it difficult to rapidly identify the true recipients of goods," Camba said.

Those challenges can be exploited to "obfuscate financial flows and potentially circumvent global restrictions on technology transfers," he added, urging constant adaptation from regulators.

Singapore said it had received limited help from U.S. authorities in its yearlong investigation.

Police sought information on Super Micro Computer Inc., a U.S. server supplier relevant to the probe, but the company provided limited cooperation, according to the Thursday statement.

The city-state first raised the issue with U.S. authorities in July 2025, and has repeated its request on several occasions since September last year. "Despite these repeated requests, Singapore has to date received limited information or assistance in respect of the company," it said.

Police interviewed Alice Huang, a Hong Kong resident who was then Megaspeed's CEO, chairperson and a key controller, in June 2025.

In February 2026, authorities concluded that there was insufficient evidence that she had committed offenses related to the declarations under investigation. She was nonetheless barred from re-entering Singapore after being found providing inaccurate information to downplay her role in Megaspeed.

The country does not enforce "unilateral export controls" imposed by other countries, the ministry said in Thursday's statement, but it expects companies operating there to take foreign export rules into account in their international business activities.

Megaspeed, which was spun off from a Chinese gaming company, became Nvidia's largest Southeast Asian customer in less than three years and has bought more than $4.8 billion worth of its chips since June 2024, according to The New York Times, though the company has denied any wrongdoing.

Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence identified as a Chinese intelligence asset passing information to Beijing, served as managing director at Megaspeed for eight months in 2023 but has reportedly continued to play a leading role at the company.

Jensen Huang has resigned as Nvidia CEO on Thursday, ending 33 years at the helm after revelations that he had two extramarital affairs, plunging the chipmaker at the heart of the global AI boom into crisis.

Nvidia appointed Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress as interim CEO while its board searches for a permanent successor.

#2879. NBA’s anti-tanking rules ironically tanking Utah Jazz season

I know that sports fans have said, "This is our year," at the beginning of every season for every sport since the dawn of man. Archaeologists only recently discovered cave drawings of ancient man calling his shot by prematurely tattooing the Larry O'Brien Trophy on his shoulder with the description: "Tigris River Valley Hunter-Gatherers 5,000 B.C. World Champions" (no need to fact-check me on this, just trust me).

But for the Utah Jazz, the 2026-27 season was actually supposed to be "their year". No blind optimism, no baseless fantasies. Just grounded, reasonable, and logical projections for a team on the rise.

Through four painful seasons, the Jazz lost a grand total of 211 games in search of an elusive young superstar to lift their tragic franchise from an endless tank. As those four seasons came to a close, Utah finally claimed their prize: a star guard out of Kansas named Darryn Peterson. And he couldn't have come at a better time, either.

Tired of witnessing one-third of the league actively trying to lose basketball games, the NBA enacted anti-tanking protocols and installed lottery reform intent on de-incentivizing the act of "tanking".

Teams have far less incentive to tank this season, especially this early on, as the owners of the four worst records will have a statistically lower chance of getting the number-one overall pick than those who finish 5th-8th from the bottom.

That adjustment is one of two reasons why Utah is off to such a slow start. The other, in my view, is having a very injured roster at a time when most teams are still playing at or near full strength. Teams are fighting to win every game, and the Jazz are ill-equipped to fight back.

As a result, the Jazz are off to an even worse start this year through 11 games than any of their rebuilding seasons. They hold hands at the bottom of the Western Conference standings with an abysmal Sacramento team.

2022-23: 8-32023-24: 4-72024-25: 3-82025-26: 4-72026-27: 1-11

The Jazz are not tanking. Not intentionally, anyway.

Utah has even less reason to minimize their competitive edge, being barred from a top-four pick after drafting second overall this summer, and owing their pick to Memphis even if it were to finish in a favorable position.

With three of their five starters out with prolonged injuries, things may get even worse before they get any better. But they can — and I believe Utah will — improve before a play-in spot is out of reach.

Assuming Lauri Markkanen, Jaren Jackson Jr. and Jusuf Nurkic all return according to current timetables, the Jazz are only a month or two away from full strength, barring further injury. This is a team still learning to play with each other. With several key pieces having arrived within the last year, chemistry will take time to develop.

A Play-In appearance is a reasonable goal this season, despite my own preseason projections anticipating a top-six finish. Should the Jazz rally and finish with a top-10 record in the Western Conference, Utah has hope of building on that success in the following season. This was never going to be "the year" for Utah. It was always destined to be a stepping stone. But so long as that stride carries the Jazz closer to a better place, there's still reason for optimism.

This is a talented team. Time will tell if talent can translate to results.

#2877. Analysis: Nvidia’s leadership crisis tests the chipmaker’s role as the ‘central bank of AI’

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's shock resignation is raising a worrying question: Could trouble at the chipmaker spread through the tightly connected financial network Nvidia has built to support the artificial intelligence boom?

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

Serious problems with Nvidia could do far-reaching economic and financial damage. The company has made hundreds of billions of dollars in financial commitments to other firms to help the industry grow, earning it the nickname the "central bank of AI." The Federal Reserve and other financial institutions are closely watching the AI buildout to monitor for the possibility that an unexpected event could destabilize the global financial system.

Even back in July Fed officials were discussing the possibility of a "broad-based repricing of assets" in the event of problems in the AI sector.

A rerun of the 2007-2008 financial crisis isn't likely, according to analysts and current and former financial officials. Nvidia isn't "too big to fail in a bank sense at all," said Sheila Bair, a former government regulator who ran the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. Unlike a troubled bank, Nvidia isn't vulnerable to a sudden loss of confidence and would still produce a product customers want to buy even if something went wrong. And its massive equity gives it a cushion.

But regulators need to watch carefully for exposure by the banks and the wider financial system to Nvidia and other firms like it, Bair said.

"Nvidia going down would be a pretty seismic event," she said.

Investors still have high hopes for Nvidia, which reports earnings on Tuesday. Analysts expect its revenue to more than double in the current fiscal year, and then to roughly double again over the following two years, according to LSEG data. But Huang's exit from the company Thursday following a pair of extramarital affairs, including one with a woman The New York Times reported was suspected of spying for China, makes the company's fate extraordinarily hard to predict. Huang had led the company since co-founding it in 1993.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment. The company named CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO.

Nvidia's stock fell 33% from its Oct. 6 peak through Thursday morning. Still, people who closely track the financial flows underlying AI say that even sharper losses by Nvidia likely wouldn't trigger the kind of systemic problems that regulators guard against.

And yet the risks flowing from AI are keeping some finance officials up at night. They are watching for the possibility that many of the factors that have helped the AI boom could reverse simultaneously. If the value of Nvidia's GPU chips fell, for instance, it could prompt investors to ask whether companies that borrowed money to buy them would be able to pay off their debts.

In that case, the financial assistance big tech firms have extended to others in the industry could suddenly become drags on their growth. Tech companies would have credit problems at the same time that their equity is falling. Problems among giant tech firms wouldn't stay contained to tech investors' portfolios, but could cause problems across the economy.

"I think that's a real concern because both the economy and financial markets are standing on the same leg, which means a drawdown could have reinforcing effects between the two," said Jason Wu, an assistant director at the International Monetary Fund who oversees the Global Markets Analysis unit.

Wu was the lead author of a chapter of the IMF's Global Financial Stability report published in October that warned about the potential for growing leverage among AI firms and investors to trigger broader problems. The so-called hyperscalers — the biggest tech firms — have grown from just 1% of bond issuance in the $15 trillion investment-grade debt market to 10% in just two years. Hedge funds and retail investors are making risky bets even riskier by taking on debt to buy stocks or investing in funds that have embedded leverage.

Nvidia is at the center of all of that. The company has repeatedly stepped in to help backstop smaller AI companies known as neoclouds, which build data centers using Nvidia chips and lease capacity to other firms. Among other obligations, Nvidia disclosed in July that it has committed to buy up to $36 billion in cloud services from those companies. That helps Nvidia ensure that there will be more customers for its AI chips, and it helps those companies find easier financing.

Wu and others refer to these kinds of arrangements as circular financing. When business is growing, it can be productive for bigger firms to help their customers buy their products. But if Nvidia couldn't honor its obligations, it would ripple through the firms that borrowed the money and the banks that lent to it. Relatively opaque private credit funds have also lent heavily into the AI boom, and how they would fare is a subject of debate among regulators.

Nvidia didn't reveal its specific partners, but some neoclouds such as CoreWeave and IREN have separately disclosed their relationships with the bigger firm.

Many neoclouds have been under stress in recent weeks. The cost of insuring CoreWeave debt, as measured by credit-default spreads, rose by 94% — nearly doubling — from Oct. 1 to Nov. 5, before settling, according to LSEG data. The price of an IREN convertible bond maturing in 2029 fell 23% from Nov. 3 to Nov. 5, amid a 26% decline in the company's share price.

Nvidia's other obligations include a $105 billion phased guarantee to a major data center project in Ohio planned by OpenAI and SB Energy, subject to certain conditions. Nvidia promised to backstop up to $125 billion of a $500 billion credit program by Wall Street financial firms.

These obligations come in different forms and are unlikely to come due all at once, if ever. Stijn Van Nieuwerburgh, a Columbia Business School finance professor, estimates them at $400 billion overall.

That is a huge sum, but still a small fraction of the roughly $3.9 trillion market capitalization Nvidia has even after its stock decline. "The company would have to get to the brink of extinction before all these guarantees come into jeopardy."

"I don't want to say it's a safe system, but I do want to say there's a lot of equity in the system right now," Van Nieuwerburgh said.

But in the past few days, Nvidia's troubles have separated it from the pack. Most neoclouds' equity prices have recovered this week as Nvidia's share price has fallen. That suggests investors see Nvidia's problems as specific to the company, for now.

Meanwhile the basic laws of economics are driving other firms to try to move into Nvidia's lucrative chips business. AMD, Broadcom, Intel, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI all have efforts underway to build their own AI chips, Van Nieuwerburgh said.

Over time, they could replace Nvidia's products. That could cap the economic fallout of any potential troubles at the company.

And yet it's impossible to entirely write off the financial risks of the AI trade suddenly hitting a wall, added Van Nieuwerburgh, who spoke to me over Zoom from the sidelines of a bimonthly meeting of finance officials in Basel, Switzerland.

Central bankers "are clearly very, very, very anxious about this and thinking hard about these questions," he said.

#2876. Barcelona’s hits and misses of the 2026-27 season so far

What a season it's been so far for Hansi Flick's Barcelona.

Notwithstanding the disappointment from the defeats against Real Madrid and Paris Saint-Germain, there's been little to reproach the team for as a collective.

Individually, some players have, very simply, been better than others, so let's take a look at the hits and misses – those who have impressed the most – and the least – for the Catalans.

There's no question that Fermin has finally cemented his place in the Barca starting XI.

There's a school of thought to suggest that he was well worthy of that long before now, but Flick will have had his reasons.

The midfielder has the energy and dynamism of Gavi, but is a much better goal threat than his contemporary.

His brace in what was a huge game against Atletico Madrid shows that when the big matches come around, Fermin can generally be relied upon to affect the game in one way or another.

It's not really a surprise that Balde finds himself out of the starting line-up.

Though he's had his moments under Hansi Flick, they've been few and far between, and the German finally snapped.

The lack of a final ball and too many runs down blind alleys have ensured that Flick has considered his options in the full-back areas.

Having pace to burn is all well and good, but if you're not actually going to do anything with it, then it's of absolutely no use.

Although everyone expects Lamine to provide match-winning moments in every game, the fact that he's managing to regularly do just that at 19 years of age is nothing short of unbelievable.

To take on that level of responsibility at such a young age, and not only not be cowed by it, but to allow it to enhance an already sensational offering is the sign of a genuine generational talent.

Already in 2026/27, Lamine has scored 13 goals in all competitions and provided 12 assists.

If he can stay free of injury for the remainder of the season, there's no telling just how important he'll be. Can he be the catalyst for a long-overdue Champions League title? Absolutely.

Barcelona signed Joao Cancelo permanently in the summer after another strong loan spell but, three months in, he's already become a problem for Flick and Co.

The Portugal international is more than capable of scoring some incredible goals, as we've seen already this season, but when it comes to the other end of the pitch he can also be a real liability at times.

Cancelo's shortcomings defensive were all too evident in the big games against Real Madrid, PSG and Atletico Madrid. With Balde out of Flick's plans, it leaves the club short of options at left-back and potentially looking for a solution in January.

There are other players who could be considered 'hits' this season, but it's the captain who sets the tone for everyone to follow.

In a team with such superstars as Rodri, Lamine and Pedri, the Brazilian's star still shines brightly, and he remains the exemplar.

His maturity, thanks to being handed the armband, has been noticeable, and there won't be too many culers arguing that he's not deserving of the honour.

With 19 goals and three assists to his name, he's leading from the front, and if his colleagues could show the same amount of desire and character as Raphinha does on a weekly basis, Barca will be untouchable.

A player who cost €80 million has done OK – as he himself has admitted – but without doing much to get culers out of their seats.

Like Balde, the Englishman runs the risk of being a player renowned for his pace and not a lot else.

Yes, it's bound to take time to settle into new surroundings, a new country etc., but Gordon, by his own admission, had always wanted to play for Barca, knew how to speak Spanish and felt comfortable from the first minute at the club.

He's not done an awful lot more than Marcus Rashford to date, and that's not intended as a criticism of either player; it's just a fact.

Gordon's work rate is fine, but he needs to up his output if he wants to be considered a success.

#2875. Marcus Smart suspended for throwing punch that made contact with Warriors player's face

Houston Rockets guard Marcus Smart has been suspended for "throwing a punch that made contact with the face" of opponent Charles Bassey during Wednesday's game against the Golden State Warriors.

The NBA released a statement on Thursday, Nov. 12 announcing the Rockets guard is being suspended three games without pay for the incident.

The night before, Smart grazed the face of Bassey when he threw a punch with 21 seconds left in the third quarter after the whistle was blown for a separate foul. Their arms had been caught in each other during a play and, when game play was stopped, Smart seemingly snapped.

After the punch was thrown, players from both teams got in a scuffle and, as the scene was broken up, the 2021-22 Defensive Player of the Year started walking to the locker room. It seemed as if he was sure he would be given a flagrant foul 2 and ejected from the game, which, after a review, he was.

Just before the incident, Smart and Bassey got tangled up under the rim when trying to box out during a Warriors possession. The two continued to jaw at each other up the floor. Then, when play halted, Smart squared up and threw a right-handed hook that nearly landed on Bassey's cheek. Bassey had moved his head backward to avoid contact. Officials later said that Smart made contact to Bassey's nose.

After the game, which the Rockets won 115-108, Bassey criticized Smart for his "fake toughness."

Houston head coach Ime Udoka also addressed the incident and shared Smart's side of the story.

"Marcus, I just talked to him, he said they got wrapped up and his elbow that he had hurt got bent up," the coach said. "And so he said something to him and Bassey said, 'What you going to do?' and he did it."

The former Oklahoma State standout will miss the Rockets' next three games due to his suspension. Houston faces the Utah Jazz, Washington Wizards and Miami Heat.

Smart's suspension will be lifted to play in the road game against the Indiana Pacers on Wednesday, Nov. 18.

Smart is averaging 8.3 points, 2.6 rebounds, 2 assists and a steal per game this season, his first with the Rockets.

He had six points, two rebounds and an assist before being booted from the Warriors matchup.

#2874. Nscale updates IPO prospectus that shows widening loss

Nvidia-backed neocloud Nscale on Thursday released updated paperwork for a U.S. initial public offering.

The company had a third-quarter net loss of $1.69 billion on $307.5 million in revenue, which was up 1,344% year over year, according to an updated prospectus for its planned initial public offering. The net loss widened from $1.02 billion in the second quarter, when the company generated $140.6 million in revenue, up 1,252%.

London-based Nscale filed to go public in September, but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company did not specify how much it intends to raise in the public offering on the New York Stock Exchange.

Since then the company has faced public setbacks.

Microsoft walked away from an Nscale data center that it had planned to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

The neocloud market, which focuses on renting access to chips such as Nvidia graphics processing units to clients, has been in tumult, partly because Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, after admitting to being unfaithful to his wife. Nvidia is investigating Huang's relationship with a woman whom intelligence agencies have called a Chinese spy.

In October investors pressured shares of Nscale rival CoreWeave, but the stock advanced almost 25% on Wednesday on strong results. CoreWeave pulled back about 7% on Thursday following the departure of Huang, who has overseen investments in the neocloud.

Shares of another neocloud, Nebius, fell about 17% on Thursday, with the company issuing light full-year guidance.

But Nscale can point to growing business with Anthropic, which itself is aiming to go public. In late September Anthropic exercised its right to contract for additional computing power at an Nscale site in West Virginia.

The Gunners already have several talented midfielders competing for places, but they continue to look for ways to strengthen their squad. De Jong would bring significant experience and another option in an area where Arsenal already have considerable depth.

Any potential move will also depend on the player's willingness to leave Barcelona. Arsenal may be interested in securing his services, but the midfielder will have to decide whether a move to London is the right next step in his career.

For now, the reported interest places De Jong among Arsenal's potential January targets as they look to maintain the strength of their squad.

#2869. Nvidia: Ex-prosecutor Joon Kim and Wachtell, Lipton bring big track record to investigation

Joon Kim, the lawyer tapped by artificial intelligence chip giant Nvidia to investigate former CEO Jensen Huang, and the Big Law firm he works for have a long history of investigating high-profile cases.

Kim, in his career as a top federal prosecutor and as an attorney in private practice, has had in his sights mobster John "The Teflon Don" Gotti's son, former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. and other high-profile people and companies.

The law firm that Kim recently joined — Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz — this year conducted an investigation for the NBA of the Los Angeles Clippers and its billionaire owner, Steve Ballmer, which ended with the basketball league imposing stiff sanctions on Ballmer and the Clippers for violating salary cap rules in connection with superstar Kawhi Leonard.

On Tuesday, Nvidia said it had retained Kim, 55, and Wachtell, Lipton to "conduct a thorough and independent investigation" of allegations that its then-CEO Jensen Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort involving his affair with a woman, Alice Huang. U.S. intelligence suspects her of being a spy for China.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO on Thursday morning on the heels of a New York Times report that he had also had an extramarital affair with an Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reports to him.

That allegation could be added to the list of issues that Kim will be looking into as part of his probe.

Nvidia did not immediately respond when asked if the parameters of the investigation would be affected by the new claim. A spokeswoman for Wachtell, Lipton declined to comment for this article on behalf of the firm and Kim.

Friends and former colleagues of Kim said Nvidia has made a smart decision in retaining him for the job.

"I think he's the ideal person for this role," said Edward Kim, a New York white-collar defense lawyer who worked in the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York with Joon Kim. The two men are not related.

"I think they're going to get a serious, credible investigation. I believe Joon will follow the facts wherever they will lead," Edward Kim said.

The son of a South Korean diplomat, Joon Kim was born in Los Angeles. He attended Phillips Exeter Academy and later Stanford University.

After graduating cum laude from Harvard Law School, he clerked for a federal judge in Manhattan and then worked as an associate at the major law firm Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton starting in the late 1990s. After that, he spent seven years in the SDNY as an assistant U.S. attorney. His tenure as a prosecutor there included four years in the Organized Crime and Terrorism Unit.

The SDNY, by virtue of its location in Manhattan, handles some of the most high-profile criminal investigations in the United States, including cases of major financial crimes, government corruption and organized crime.

During his first stint at the SDNY, Kim investigated and prosecuted crimes that included "racketeering, murder, money laundering, securities fraud, firearms and narcotics offenses, tax evasion, and terrorism," the office has previously said.

Kim's trial wins at the SDNY included obtaining a conviction of Peter Gotti, then-head of the Gambino Mafia family, for conspiring to kill notorious mob turncoat Salvatore "Sammy the Bull" Gravano. Kim and his fellow prosecutors were less successful, however, in prosecuting Peter Gotti's nephew, John Gotti Jr., whose trials ended with several hung juries.

"He could be a very tenacious courtroom advocate," said Chris Conniff, a New York attorney who worked with Kim in the SDNY, and who considers Kim one of his closest friends.

"He's incredibly smart," Conniff said. "I think he's known by everyone in the business as just an outstanding investigator, leaves no stone unturned."

Kim in 2006 returned to Cleary Gottlieb as a partner in the firm's litigation and enforcement group but then went back to the SDNY in 2013. During his second stint there, he served as deputy U.S. attorney, and then served as acting U.S. attorney from 2017 to 2018.

Kim's elevation to acting U.S. attorney came as a result of President Donald Trump's firing of then-U.S. Attorney Preet Bharara in March 2017 after Bharara refused the new president's demand that he resign.

"Preet's departure was very unique," and the mood in the SDNY "was shaky" after his ouster, Conniff said.

"Preet was very well-liked and well-respected," said Conniff, who was in private practice at the time that Bharara was fired. "We just lost a very charismatic and effective leader, and in a very dramatic way."

In taking over from Bharara, Kim, "from my external viewpoint, he righted the ship and continued to do the work of the office in the time he was there."

"Joon, I would say, gave people confirmation that it was going to be alright, and it was going to be business as usual," Conniff said.

Kim left the SDNY in April 2018, and began his third stint at Cleary Gottlieb, which he recently left to join Wachtell, Lipton's litigation department.

In March 2021, Kim and another attorney, Anne Clark, a partner at Vladeck, Raskin & Clark, were tapped by New York Attorney General Letitia James to investigate then-Gov. Andrew Cuomo over allegations that he had sexually harassed several women.

"These are serious allegations that demand a rigorous and impartial investigation," Kim said in a statement at the time. "We will act judiciously and follow the facts wherever they lead."

Kim and Clark in August 2021 submitted a 165-page report to James, which concluded that Cuomo had sexually harassed at least 11 women and then retaliated against a former employee who had publicly complained about his behavior. As part of the probe, Kim, Clark and their team interviewed 179 witnesses and reviewed tens of thousands of documents.

Cuomo adamantly denied some of the allegations and said that other claims of alleged misconduct had been mischaracterized or misinterpreted.

A week after the report was publicly released, Cuomo announced that he would resign.

Two years after the Cuomo probe, Kim was retained by the FDIC to conduct an independent review of allegations of sexual harassment and interpersonal misconduct at the agency. As part of the probe, Kim and his team conducted 167 interviews of current and past FDIC employees, and hundreds of additional interviews with individuals who contacted a hotline the team set up for employees to report misconduct claims.

An April 2024 report by Kim and his team found "for far too many employees and for far too long, the FDIC has failed to provide a workplace safe from sexual harassment, discrimination, and other interpersonal misconduct."

More than five years after Cuomo left office, the former governor's spokesman has not forgotten, or forgiven, Kim, whom he accused of writing a report on Cuomo with a predetermined outcome.

Kim was a "hired hack," said the spokesman, Rich Azzopardi.

Azzopardi said Kim "hid evidence" that was exonerating of Cuomo, and accepted at face value "outright lies" from a number of witnesses.

The bad blood between Cuomo's camp and Kim predates the sexual harassment probe.

While working in the SDNY, Kim and Bharara oversaw the investigation that led to the corruption trial of Joseph Percoco, who had been a close aide of Cuomo. The trial took place after both prosecutors had left the office.

Azzopardi cited that prosecution in his scathing remarks about Kim.

Percoco was sentenced to six years in prison after his 2018 conviction for honest-services wire fraud. The conviction was overturned by the U.S. Supreme Court in a unanimous ruling in 2023 — after his release from prison — because the judge's instructions to jurors were too vague.

Azzopardi said that Kim and Bharara were motivated to prosecute Percoco for "their own personal glory" and "to generate headlines for themselves."

Bharara, in a two-word email response about Azzopardi's claim, wrote, "Andrew who?," referring to Cuomo.

Azzopardi said he did not believe there was any other reason for Nvidia's board to retain Kim to investigate the Huang situation other than to reach a predetermined outcome by the board.

"I don't know what the aims of the board are," Azzopardi said.

Kim's friend Conniff, when asked if he expected that the Nvidia investigation would be a whitewash that would serve the board's interests, said, "No."

"I don't think Joon would take an engagement in which he could not provide a fulsome report of what he uncovered during this investigation," Conniff said. "Any company board that is looking to do a good, thorough investigation of potential wrongdoing, Joon's perfect ... for it."
