#2868. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2867. Luke McCaffrey knew the rules and made a heads-up play, causing a kickoff out of bounds

Commanders kickoff returner Luke McCaffrey made the smartest play of the game by not returning a kick in Thursday night's win over the Giants.

With McCaffrey back to field a Giants kickoff, the ball landed near the sideline at the 5-yard line. The kick stayed in bounds, and some returners would have grabbed the ball and started running. But McCaffrey let the ball bounce, then stepped out of bounds, and then grabbed the ball with his feet down on the sideline.

By rule, the ball is out of bounds if it touches a player who is out of bounds, even if the ball actually stays in bounds. By touching the ball while his feet were out of bounds, McCaffrey forced a kickoff out of bounds penalty on the Giants, putting the ball on the 40-yard line for the Commanders.

"I thought Luke McCaffrey made a really smart play that won't show up on the stat sheet," Commanders head coach Dan Quinn said. "That's a difficult play. Is it out of bounds? Is it not? I was nervous about it, that's one of the ones where you hold your breath."

Every kickoff returner needs to know that rule, but knowing the rule is easier than having the quick-thinking ability to do what McCaffrey did, in the fourth quarter with the game on the line. It was among the smartest football plays you'll see.

#2866. In a policy switch, the Fed says its October 2027 meeting will not see a vote on interest rates

The Federal Reserve won't vote on a potential interest-rate change at its October 2027 meeting, a departure that is part of a broader initiative from Chairman Kevin Warsh aimed at reducing the central bank's market footprint.

According to an announcement Thursday, the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee will set aside its Oct. 26-27 session for "the discussion of broad monetary policy strategy issues."

Warsh announced the move at his news conference after the FOMC met in October, though he did not specify which meeting would be affected.

In 2027, there will be seven voting meetings, with the October session set up as extra time for policy deliberation without a formal vote or post-meeting statement. The new calendar shows that the final meeting of the year will now take place in November. It had previously been set for December.

"Now, what's the reasoning behind this change? To me, it's quite straightforward," the chairman said in explaining his rationale. "Getting policy right involves assessing near-term developments at timely, regular, and information-rich intervals."

"But it's equally important we reserve time and space, as an intellectually diverse group, for strategic deliberations—for thinking anew about big questions, looking out over longer time horizons, focusing, as I say, on the left side of the decimal point," he said.

Warsh has been critical of the Fed's prior communications strategy, saying policymakers spent too much time providing guidance on policy moves that handcuffed them when it came time to make decisions.

Under typical practice, the Fed meets eight times a year, following with a vote on interest rates and then a news conference from the chair.

Since Warsh took over, the post-meeting statement has been shortened dramatically. Moreover, the press question-and-answer sessions have been cut to around half an hour from their customary 45 minutes or so.

Warsh said in October that the meeting schedule is another step in his plan to streamline communication. He indicated last month that the number of nonvoting meetings would increase to two in 2028.

"I believe the setting of the meeting schedule and agenda is more than some administrative detail," he said. "It's an institutional choice, affecting the nature and the quality of our deliberations and, ultimately, the wisdom of our decisions. It's yet another change to get the conduct of monetary policy on the right track."

The FOMC next meets Dec. 8-9. Its first meeting in 2027 will happen Jan. 26-27.

Correction: The Fed's October 2027 meeting will not see a vote on interest rates. The headline in an earlier version misstated the year.

#2865. No. 2 Michigan Women’s Basketball wears down Columbia in 81-58 road win

The No. 2 Michigan women's basketball team has a goal to hold each opponent below 15 points per quarter. The Wolverines know if that lofty goal is completed, their offense can generally surpass those 60 points to secure a win.

Buoyed by its consistent defense, Michigan had two key stretches — a 7-0 run in the first quarter to establish its 10-point cushion, and a 16-5 third quarter burst — to seal the game. Those runs pushed the second-ranked Wolverines (3-0) to their first road win of the 2026-27 campaign, defeating Columbia (3-1), 81-58. Junior guard Syla Swords led the scoring with 17 points, followed by 15 apiece from junior guard Olivia Olson and junior forward Kendall Dudley.

"The better team sometimes can wear you down and wear you out," head coach Kim Barnes Arico said. "And I knew that we would probably be able to do that. And I was hoping we would be able to do that at the start of the third quarter. And that kind of happened for us. I also think we got super stagnant in the second quarter, and we didn't share the ball enough. The ball wasn't moving enough, and that's something we talked about at halftime."

Michigan's transition-based offense struggled to score fast break points against Columbia in the first half. The Lions got back in transition whether or not they made a basket, forcing the Wolverines to execute in the half-court. Passing and off-ball movement stagnated early, resulting in one-on-one actions and guards driving to the basket in isolation.

That offensive approach worked to an extent, lapsing for stretches but also connecting in the mid-range. The Wolverines largely stagnated in the second quarter besides Dudley, whose nine points of Michigan's 13 in the frame prevented Columbia from closing the gap. Dudley's final 15-point, 10-rebound statline bolstered the Wolverines, marking her first career double-double. Swords, a 3-point threat who is often ran off the line, drove for pullup jumpers to help Michigan separate, leading 36-26 at half.

"We were really clunky," Barnes Arico said. "… I thought we tried to attack more one-on-one than moving the ball. I thought when we moved the ball and we used screens, we got some slips, we got some really good looks. We just weren't patient, and I think part of it is staying patient in those moments because we are such a high transition team. We're used to scoring so quickly. If we don't score quickly, you know, can we be able to execute in the half court? And it's our third game, I think we're not nearly a finished product in that space."

At halftime, the Wolverines adjusted in the half-court, moving the ball more and creating cleaner looks. The changes created a 10-2 run beginning with Michigan's first second-half possession, reversing the ball and opening up the floor in the half-court. Layup attempts were products of well-spaced cuts rather than isolation drives into traffic, yielding better conversion rates. The 10-2 run continued into the 16-5 outburst, leading 52-31 after a three-point play from Olson with 4:51 remaining in the quarter.

Michigan cooled off in the half-court, but the Wolverines' press generated some turnovers and transition buckets, maintaining the cushion even as Lions guard Riley Weiss started getting open looks. Weiss went for 23 points, including 7-for-8 shooting from the free throw line, despite being circled on the scouting report as the reigning Ivy League Player of the Year.

"I wasn't really happy on the defensive end, but you know we forced 21 turnovers and they shot 32.8 percent," Barnes Arico said. "That's actually pretty good when I reflect. Riley Weiss is a really good player. … Where we fouled her, we're late on some stuff and put her to the free throw line. I think that's another area where we're trying to get better too, is not fouling people and putting them to the free throw line."

In the season-opening win against Connecticut, Michigan largely avoided foul trouble and limited the Huskies to seven shots from the charity stripe. The Wolverines have struggled to keep opponents off the line since then, surrendering 15-for-18 shooting to the Lions from the line — more than Columbia scored in each of three quarters.

Certainly, the road game exposed some things for Michigan to clean up — half-court offense, limiting shooting fouls, three-point shooting (4-for-18) — yet the Wolverines wore down the Lions and strung together enough dominant stretches to separate by 23 in the end.

#2864. Lakers newcomer learns to trust franchise’s belief in him

MILWAUKEE — The Lakers have been getting more from Sandro Mamukelashvili recently than simply his highest-scoring games since joining the franchise over the summer.

The 23 points he scored in Monday's win over the Hawks and Wednesday's victory over the Bucks were necessary during games they were either without or lost key players during the games.

Sandro Mamukelashvili of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Detroit Pistons on Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026, at Little Caesars Arena in Detroit. NBAE via Getty Images

But what the Lakers hope can be taken from those games beyond Mamukelashvili's scoring output was his offensive approach.

"High performance — a few of the key components to that is belief, focus and freedom," coach JJ Redick said. "And I want him to understand how much his teammates, his coaches, myself, we believe in him."

Sandro Mamukelashvili (54) of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots against the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, at Fiserv Forum in Milwaukee. NBAE via Getty Images

The sixth-year forward feels that belief from his current teammates during his first season with the Lakers.

It's a catalyst of Mamukelashvili having the performance he's had recently where he's been active in looking for ways he can contribute offensively.

"I want to be aggressive from the jump," he said. "I know these guys trust me, I know Coach trusts me. He wants me to be me. He wants to just let me play how I'm used to playing. So, it just kind of like eases my mind, doesn't make me think that much."

That's how the Lakers want him to operate.

Redick referred to Mamukelashvili being an "over-thinker" during training camp — which the Lakers coach could relate to.

Which is why Redick's made it an emphasis to help the 27-year-old realize they want him to play more freely.

"The big thing for him is understanding that he has a lot of freedom," Redick said. "Luka [Doncic], [Austin Reaves] — that doesn't need to be said — they have freedom within the structure of our offense. Mamu also has a lot of freedom."

Sandro Mamukelashvili of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Detroit Pistons on Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026, at Little Caesars Arena in Detroit. NBAE via Getty Images

Mamukelashvili, who played for the Bucks, Spurs and Raptors before joining the Lakers, recognized how different this is compared with his former teams.

"It was a hard journey from a two-way [contract] and out of the rotation," he said. "I feel like it mentally kind of drains you. It kind of sometimes makes you doubt yourself, makes you think about what you can do out there to help the team win and everywhere you have kind of a different challenge.

"I feel like here, they just tell me, 'We want you to be you. We want you to be a big part of this team. And just believe in yourself.' I have never had a chance to have somebody who would tell me that. It started a little bit last year, but I feel like this year, I feel much more freedom."

Mamukelashvili, who's averaging 10.8 points this season, acknowledged it's a process of realizing the trust he has.

"I hope I'll get better," he said. "I hope I'll start believing in myself as much as they do. I think I do. But as I said, sometimes going through all this up and down you're like, 'Do they really trust me?' Like, you start questioning it. And once you start assuming what people think, how they want you to play, that's when you lose your swagger. So I just got to be me and go out and keep playing with confidence."

#2863. Did Giants beat Commanders? Final score of NFL 'Thursday Night Football' game last night

If you did, well, you missed quite the finish on "Thursday Night Football" between the Washington Commanders and New York Giants. A first-half snooze fest quickly picked up in the second half as the teams traded blows in another tightly contested NFC East battle.

With the Commanders fighting for their playoff lives, it came down to the wire once again. It wasn't an unfamiliar spot for the visitors, who've faced that situation more than enough this season. Except this time, they were trying to flip the script.

That was never going to be easy against the Giants, who have been able to win the close games since John Harbaugh took over as head coach.

The Commanders' season would've been on life support with a loss, putting them four games back of the Giants without owning the tiebreaker.

Instead the Commanders now own the tiebreaker and are just two games behind the NFC East-leading Giants.

The NFC East is now wide open: The Giants came into the night with a chance to essentially eliminate the Commanders from postseason contention. New York would've owned the tiebreaker with a win and held a four-game lead over Washington. Instead, it's Washington that now has the tiebreaker. And Dan Quinn's team is only two games back. With the Cowboys still lurking, the Giants can no longer feel comfortable atop the NFC East. In some ways, this race is just getting started.

The Giants' special teams are far from special: It's easy to point to J.J. McCarthy's interception at the end or how the loss of Jaxson Dart is being felt. Special teams was the difference in this one though. Dominic Zvada, the rookie kicker, missed a 33-yard field goal and had a kickoff out of bounds. Jordan Stout's 12-yard punt gave the Commanders good field position on the go-ahead touchdown drive. It was Zvada that missed multiple kicks in Washington that helped lead to a tie. The Commanders now own the tiebreaker instead of splitting the series. That could loom large as the season heads down the stretch.

Jayden Daniels reminded everyone of who he is: He tossed two uncharacteristic interceptions, but Daniels appeared to be in control all night long. He was active with his legs and efficient through the air, while showing plenty of poise on the go-ahead touchdown drive. Daniels has been the subject of jokes all season long for his late-game failures. On this night, it was Daniels that got the lost laugh.

#2862. How the Jensen Huang affair changes the outlook for U.S.-China relations

BEIJING — Prolonged U.S. Congressional scrutiny of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy raises the stakes for already tense relations between the world's two largest economies.

Nvidia's Huang resigned Thursday, and agreed to testify before Congress sometime before Thanksgiving on Nov. 26. House Foreign Affairs Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., told reporters there could be "public hearings for other people or other things down road."

"I worry this incident will deteriorate the atmosphere dramatically in Washington against China, and also export control matters, and also the Trump administration in its way of dealing with China," said a Beijing-based foreign policy researcher, who requested anonymity as academics risk discipline or even removal for speaking to the media.

The researcher warned of a possible return to a situation like that in the 1990s, when a "Chinese-American" nuclear physicist was suspected of sharing nuclear secrets with the Chinese government, resulting in a federal prosecution. The court ultimately dismissed all but one of 59 counts.

Now the bilateral relationship, supported by a fragile trade truce, faces another espionage allegation. And this time it's around the tech executive at the heart of the U.S.-China competition over artificial intelligence.

"This isn't really about Jensen," said Mitch Presnick, non-resident honorary fellow on the Chinese economy, Center for China Analysis at the Asia Society.

He noted how some in Washington prefer measures to exclude China and disagreed with the semiconductor executive's message that American technology needed to be used in China in order to remain relevant to global tech development.

Washington started to restrict Nvidia's ability to sell advanced chips to China in 2022. But the Trump administration eased many Biden-era restrictions in 2025.

Meanwhile, Nvidia's Huang advocated his views across a spectrum of markets from the U.S. to China to Taiwan to Germany.

At the peak of his influence, he shared a banquet table in September with U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping at a summit in Washington, D.C. The two presidents are next expected to meet alongside the APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting from Nov. 18 to 19 in Shenzhen, China.

The political landscape has swiftly changed, with Democrats sweeping U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3 and turmoil over subsequent reports about Huang's alleged Beijing spy connection. Jensen Huang admitted to an affair over the weekend.

It remains unclear whether any sensitive information was shared.

China's Foreign Ministry has called the "Chinese spy" allegation unfounded "smears against China."

"The U.S. should stop fabricating the "Chinese spy" narratives as a pretext for imposing technology restrictions," spokeswoman Mao Ning said Thursday, according to an official English translation.

But the immediate outcome, analysts predict, is more of the U.S. restrictions that China doesn't want.

"The Nvidia scandal has destroyed the credibility of the biggest corporate lobbyist against U.S. export controls on China," said Arthur Kroeber, partner, Gavekal Dragonomics. "There is now a good chance that Congress will tighten up those rules before the end of this year."

Looking ahead, Kroeber warned Beijing could cut off supplies of rare earths or chipmaking materials if the U.S. tried to restrict China further with the MATCH Act or Affiliates Rule. The measures could, respectively, limit Beijing's access to key tech suppliers in the Netherlands and Japan, and drastically expand export controls to include many subsidiaries.

"So when Trump and Xi meet next week in Shenzhen they will probably agree to keep the uneasy U.S.-China truce going," Kroeber said. "It will just keep getting more and more uneasy."

Pressuring China has been a rare area of bipartisan agreement in Washington. Beijing has meanwhile ramped up homegrown alternatives to U.S. tech since Washington restricted Nvidia's ability to sell advanced chips to China in 2022. And even Trump's approval of some Nvidia sales had yet to translate into meaningful orders from China.

"We are going to see the two countries start to decouple again in terms of technology," George Chen, partner and chair of digital practice, The Asia Group, said Thursday, noting "China will double-down on self-reliance."

"I begin to feel the Jensen Huang case feels like more like another 'spy balloon' case," he said.

In early 2023, during the Biden administration, the U.S. shot down what it described as a Chinese surveillance balloon. Beijing called it a weather balloon that had blown off course. But the incident delayed then-U.S. Secretary of State Antony Blinken's trip to China by more than four months, following limited engagement amid Covid-19 travel restrictions.

Fast-forward to the Huang affair, and "the timing is no accident here," Presnick said. "We're talking about a very curious set of coincidences, and also the fact that the information came through unnamed sources, and there were other things that were strange about it."

Days after the U.S. midterms, The Financial Times and The New York Times reported, citing sources, that U.S. government intelligence believe China's Ministry of State Security recruited businesswoman Alice Huang to gather information on then-CEO of Nvidia Jensen Huang.

"Obviously somebody was looking to do this, and they wanted to ... make the discussion about espionage, and not about American competitiveness," Presnick said.

"The U.S. is thinking [in terms of] U.S. and China, but that's not what China is thinking," he said, pointing to Beijing's trade with fast-growing non-Western economies in Africa and Southeast Asia.

—CNBC's Samantha Subin contributed to this report.

#2861. Count on him: Erik Sandvik finds his footing at Wyoming

Years ago, Utah kicker Andy Phillips put that reminder on my radar. It became a fun rallying cry for the guys whose work tends to get taken for granted right up until a football goes somewhere it shouldn't. It even earned its own hashtag on Twitter.

I brought it up to Erik Sandvik after Wyoming's overtime win at San Jose State on Oct. 9. The Cowboys' kicker had made every field goal he had attempted that season, including the one that had just sent his teammates into celebration.

"I mean, honestly, it wasn't that hard of a kick," Sandvik said.

You will learn that modesty is one of Sandvik's endearing qualities.

The winner was from 21 yards. The distance was manageable. The consequences were considerably larger. Wyoming had arrived in San Jose at 2-3, a week removed from being shut out at North Dakota State. Sandvik's kick delivered a 16-13 victory and started a winning streak that has now reached four games.

The latest installment came this past weekend in Las Vegas, where the Cowboys held off UNLV 41-35 to become bowl eligible for the first time under Jay Sawvel and the first time since 2023. Wyoming is 6-3 overall, 5-1 in Mountain West play and alone in second place in the conference.

A month ago, the Cowboys were trying to get their season moving. Now they have a postseason destination to look forward to and a conference race to help settle.

Sandvik has been doing his part to keep the itinerary interesting.

He made all three field goals in San Jose, connecting from 38 and 29 yards before the overtime finish. With his extra point, he accounted for 10 of Wyoming's 16 points and earned Mountain West Special Teams Player of the Week honors.

Against UNLV, he went 2-for-2 on field goals and 5-for-5 on extra points, contributing 11 points. His 43-yarder broke a 7-7 tie in the first quarter. His 35-yarder as the first half expired turned a late interception into three more points.

Two field goals. Six points. A six-point victory.

That arithmetic deserves a little attention, too.

"I don't worry about the scoreboard though," Sandvik shared. "I just go out there and kick the ball."

This fall has offered plenty of reasons to appreciate the players who execute in high-pressure situations. Through Week 10, Texas A&M's David Olano was 16-for-17 on field goals, including 4-for-4 from at least 50 yards, with a long of 57. Fresno State's Manaki Watanabe was 15-for-18, also perfect on four attempts from 50-plus yards. His September visit to San Jose State included makes from 52, 51, 44 and 40 yards.

The punters have supplied their own arguments. When Texas A&M's Tyler White was named to the Ray Guy Award midseason watch list on Nov. 5, he was averaging 49.4 yards per punt, with 17 of his 29 attempts traveling at least 50 yards.

Wyoming has a pretty good example in its own specialist room. Bart Edmiston averaged 55 yards on three punts against UNLV, all three traveling at least 50 yards. Through nine games, the Cowboys were averaging 48.7 yards per punt.

There is a lot to appreciate about a player who can leave an opponent a longer field, or salvage points from a drive that has run out of answers.

For a team working its way out of a stumbling start, those contributions can keep a game within reach long enough for something to change.

In Wyoming's case, the importance was right there in the arithmetic in San Jose. Sandvik supplied points the Cowboys could not afford to leave behind.

But the person behind those points had taken a much longer route to that moment than a 21-yard kick suggested.

Sandvik played soccer through his senior year of high school in Steamboat Springs, Colorado. He said he did not begin working with a kicking coach until after his junior year. He tried camps. Nothing came of them.

"I was planning on just going to CU Boulder to just go to school," he said.

Then came Wyoming's specialist camp, where Sandvik said he went 10-for-10 and earned an opportunity to join a program that already had John Hoyland.

A perfect day got him a chance. The next few years taught him what to do with it.

He redshirted in 2023 and backed up Hoyland in 2024. Last season, his first as Wyoming's starting kicker, he finished 6-for-11 on field goals with a long of 41 yards.

That makes what he's done this fall worth a little extra attention.

Sandvik made his first eight attempts through the San Jose State game, then two more against Northern Illinois. His first miss came against Air Force on Oct. 23. He responded in Las Vegas by making both attempts.

Through nine games, he is 12-for-13 on field goals, converting 92.3% of his attempts, and 23-for-23 on extra points. That adds up to 59 points.

The perfect streak ended. The progress didn't disappear with it.

A year after making six field goals all season, Sandvik has doubled that total with three regular-season games remaining. The kicker who once planned to head to Boulder as a student has become someone Wyoming can send onto the field with a game waiting to be decided.

His explanation for that development involves a little conversation.

"So in my head, I count my steps back," Sandvik said.

One, two, three moving backward. One, two to the side. A look at the uprights.

Those are the instructions. The routine gives him a sequence to follow when everyone else is thinking about what happens next.

There is something wonderfully unglamorous about it. The game-winning moment is built out of steps, reminders and repetitions that never make a highlight reel.

Long snapper Nathan Curry and holder Gavyn Helm have work to do before Sandvik gets to finish his. Their names rarely receive the same attention as the player credited with the points, but Sandvik knows how much depends on them.

"I trust both of them," he said. "Being able to trust them makes my job super easy."

The operation works well enough that the guy at the end can describe it as easy. That is a compliment to everyone involved.

The celebration, however, could use a little more preparation.

"We need to come up with a better handshake," Sandvik admitted after the San Jose State win.

A reasonable action item for the next special teams meeting. Four consecutive wins should at least provide some opportunities to practice.

Wyoming's larger turnaround has been built on getting more of those individual jobs right. After averaging 14.2 points over their first five games, the Cowboys have averaged 31.5 during their four-game winning streak, scoring 41 twice.

The UNLV finish required contributions from across the roster. Starting quarterback Tyler Hughes missed the second half with an injury. Tyson Shamsid-Deen intercepted a pass in the end zone with 50 seconds left, and Wyoming's running game helped put the result away.

Sandvik had already supplied his 11 points. Edmiston had delivered his three long punts. The defense and offense finished the work.

"Our players stayed in it and executed at the most critical time," Sawvel said in Wyoming's postgame report.

The Cowboys still have UTEP, New Mexico, and UConn on the regular-season schedule. Bowl eligibility is secured. There is more to play for.

And Sandvik helped supply some of the confidence that got them here.

After the San Jose State win, he credited the offense for putting him in position and the defense for its stops. Asked about leadership within the specialist group, he described a collective approach.

"We all take each other's opinions into consideration, and we all respect each other."

That is the part worth remembering when the conversation turns to whether a kick should be automatic.

There is a person taking those steps back. There are two more handling the snap and hold. There are years of work behind a few seconds that everyone watching expects to go perfectly.

Sandvik knows what a miss feels like. He also knows what it feels like to give his teammates a reason to celebrate.

He feels it all, because kickers are people too.

#2860. Sixers take care of business without LeBron, secure 10th vs. Raptors

Maybe not the blowout people are looking for, but still a pretty decent win. The Sixers erased a 17-point first half deficit to defeat the Toronto Raptors 123-112 Thursday night.

Tyrese Maxey led the Sixers with 27 shooting 8-of-13 from the field along with eight assists. Joel Embiid didn't quite break out of his shooting slump, but looked much better as he avoided an injury scare early in the game. He finished with 20 points going 7-of-17 from the floor along with four rebounds.

Jaylen Brown had an up-and-down night, going 8-of-15 from the field for 18 points and six rebounds. VJ Edgecombe had another great swiss army knife game, finishing with 15 points, eight rebounds and eight assists. R.J. Barrett led all scorers with 31.

LeBron James was out for this one with lower back management, as well as Adem Bona (neck) and Dominick Barlow (back). Toronto was without Kawhi Leonard, Scottie Barnes, and Trayce Jackson-Davis.

The Sixers opened the game generating good shots. Brown shook free of CMB for a pull-up jumper, Dean Wade and Edgecombe both stepped into open threes, and Embiid got a jumper from the free throw line, but none of these looks fell. Maxey finally got the Sixers their first field goal of the game getting the ball off a broken play and getting to the basket.

Embiid set a screen for Maxey to help him get open on the three-point line and appeared to bang knees with Ja'Kobe Walter as Walter was trying to fight through the screen. Embiid went down in a heap of pain immediately and the Sixers stopped the game so he could head back to the locker room.

The Sixers' offense continued to struggle after the stoppage. Brown was trying attack off the dribble but was getting bottled up. They still couldn't hit a three off the catch either — the rest of the team started the game 0-of-4 from deep. The Raptors ripped off a 12-0 run during this stretch.

For the second game in a row, Edgecombe had a really nice spin move to split defenders and get to the basket. Darko Rajakovich called timeout after he did so, which allowed Embiid to emerge from the locker room and return to the game. Edgecombe did just about the same thing coming out of the timeout.

Maxey ran quick off a miss to create a fast break layup, but Toronto was able to snuff out the momentum quickly. The Sixers went into a zone for a couple of possessions, but the Raptors shooters had gotten into a rhythm and they were able to get open pretty easily. Two threes from Barrett and another from Jamison Battle helped them score 13 more unanswered points.

Embiid nearly passed up a wide open layup near the basket, but did eventually put it up once he realized the 6-foot-1 Jamal Shead was guarding him for his first field goal of the game. Maxey was able to hit a ridiculous layup in transition, and Anfernee Simons drilled a three at the buzzer to pull the Sixers to within nine.

Simons drilled a stepback three with a hand in his face to open the quarter. Embiid, looking a bit more comfortable, blocked a layup and got himself an easy bucket going to the paint. Brown wasn't able to bury his three after making Kyle Anderson fall with a crossover, but he finally got a midrange to go that capped off a 15-0 Sixers run going back to the first.

That seemed to do the trick as Brown drilled his next two pull-ups in the midrange. Edgecombe was the best Sixer on the floor for much of the first half. His intercepting of passes from the weak side was the best defense they had going for them, and it was creating plenty of transition opportunities. On top of a big fast break dunk, Edgecombe also hit a very nifty up-and-under layup.

It probably gave everyone a good scare, but Embiid was able to get by Jakob Poeltl and throw down a dunk, albeit one that did not look like the smoothest landing. The Sixers' offense started scorching, but a lack of stops prevented them from building a lead. Nick Nurse called a timeout to regroup after they allowed two offensive rebounds on one possession, ending with a putback.

That timeout also gave Nurse a chance to put Maxey back in the game, who immediately went straight to the basket and got fouled. After teams traded threes, Maxey got to the hoop again, but Embiid picked up his third foul of the night trying to grab a defensive rebound and had to sit for the last two and a half minutes of the half.

Brown took up the mantle offensively, nailing another pull-up and driving for a layup. Jabari Walker, who didn't have much of an impact in his first shift, picked up an offensive rebound and drew a foul pulling down another. His putback was answered with another three from Barrett. Wade missed another open three on the ensuing possession, keeping the game tied at 60 at the break.

Both teams stayed hot coming out of the break as Wade and Edgecombe each drilled a corner three. On the other end of the floor the Raptors drove right to the basket for three easy layups. Immanuel Quickley also drilled a very deep, very contested pull-up three. Embiid missed a fadeaway over Poeltl, but got himself another easy dunk playing high-low with Edgecombe.

The Sixers were still giving up plenty of easy baskets off simple actions, but they were guarding one-on-one better when the Raptors isolated. Brown blocked a Barrett layup and jumped a passing lane for a pick-six. Screens ended up being risky plays on the night as Kentavious Caldwell-Pope came up favoring his shoulder trying to fight through one, but he didn't have to leave the game.

Despite throwing a post-entry pass for Brown a little too far, Embiid seemed to finding his comfort offensively. He was confident in his physical advantages over Poeltl and was aggressive because of that. It did seem like he was trying to dunk as much as he could, which did lead to him fumbling away an attempt at the basket.

Embiid is still waiting on that midrange game to come back around as he was long on another free throw line jumper. It remained a hot Simons game, something the team desperately needed with how they've been shooting lately.

Maxey was a consistent source of scoring to close the quarter, but stops on the other end continued to come at a premium. The Sixers were finally able to get some space after Quickley missed a pretty open layup after beating Maxey through the paint. Simons got it up the floor for a transition basket. The Raptors called a timeout to regroup, but CMB got called for a moving screen on the first play coming out of it. Maxey got to the line, which Barrett responded to by backing him down in the post for a layup, bringing the Sixers lead back down to nine after three.

Walker opened the quarter with a corner three and just missed a second a few possessions later. He did scoop up another putback after Edgecombe's attempt to dunk on two Raptors came up empty, and Simons swiped the ball away from Barrett for a fast break bucket to start the quarter strong.

At some point in the first half, the ball got stuck behind the backboard and the crowd let Poeltl hear it as he struggled to knock it free with a broom. When the ball got stuck again in the fourth, a referee brought the broom back to Poeltl on the bench for a chance at redemption. The crowd got a kick out of it, but Poeltl did not, and refused. The crowd shared a sarcastic celebration with KCP after it took him a few attempts to dislodge it.

The Sixers' offense seemed to hit a bit of a lull midway through the fourth. Brown missed a pair of free throws and was blocked again at the rim by CMB. Edgecombe tried to hit Brown with a lob but that was broken up by CMB again, but Edgecombe was able to pick it back up and finish it himself. After giving up second chance points on the following possession, Nurse called a timeout to put Embiid and Maxey back in the game.

The Raptors got another basket inside, a trip to the line, and a corner three to cut the lead down to six with plenty of time left. Simons hit a much needed three, as was the case for much of the night. Embiid finally knocked down a midrange after a couple of stops, but Quickley walked into an open three off a screen on the next trip down the floor.

Maxey got to the line again as the Raptors started to run out of time. Embiid got past a couple of defenders and nearly had another self lob off the backboard, but that was also broken up by CMB. Embiid was still able to secure the ball and get it to KCP, who buried a three right before the shot clock expired. Embiid hit a midrange on the following possession, wrapping up this game quite nicely for both himself and the team.

The double-digit lead gave the fans enough confidence for loud "We Want Tacko" chants, and Nurse obliged, putting Tacko Fall in for the final 54 seconds of the game. Poor Jabari Walker got a round of boos when he went to the scorer's table on account of not being Tacko Fall.

#2859. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2858. Cisco beats on earnings, says AI infrastructure revenue will double this fiscal year

Cisco shares rose in extended trading on Thursday after the maker of networking gear reported better-than-expected fiscal first-quarter results.

Here's how the company did compared to LSEG consensus estimates:

Revenue increased 24% from $14.88 billion a year earlier, Cisco said in a statement. Net income rose to $4.6 billion, or $1.14 per share, from $2.9 billion, or 72 center per share, a year ago.

Cisco is trying to gain a foothold in the artificial intelligence buildout after missing out on the early years of the generative AI boom. The company's stock price is up about 35% this year as of Thursday's close, trouncing the Nasdaq, which has gained 9% over that stretch.

Cisco raised its expectation for AI infrastructure revenue from hyperscalers to $9 billion this fiscal year, more than double the figure in fiscal 2026.

"Organizations are turning to Cisco for our trusted, market-defining technology and our expertise to help them scale, secure and optimize their environments for the agentic AI era," CEO Chuck Robbins said in the statement.

For the fiscal second quarter, Cisco said it expects revenue of between $18 billion and $18.2 billion, topping the average analyst estimate of $17.88 billion, according to LSEG. The company is projecting adjusted earnings per share of $1.34 to $1.36, beating the $1.23 average estimate.

#2856. Would you be willing to do a bad contract swap trade?

Earlier this week, Bob Nightengale of USA Today mentioned that the Phillies have made Trea Turner available in a trade. Of course, that doesn't really mean much, as it would be almost impossible to move the underperforming Turner and the remaining seven years and roughly $200M he is still owed, and that is before factoring in his full no-trade clause. We have to acknowledge that reality before we even entertain the idea of any theoretical trade.

With that said, nothing is truly impossible. Bad contract swaps are uncommon in MLB, but they do happen. One such trade occurred just last offseason when the Mets sent Brandon Nimmo to the Texas Rangers in exchange for Marcus Semien. Nimmo at the time was still owed over $100M over five years of an eight year, $162M extension that he signed with New York prior to the 2023 season while Semien was entering the fifth year of a seven year, $175M deal he inked with the Rangers before 2022. Both players were coming off of disappointing seasons and were on the wrong side of 30, with Nimmo being 32 at the time and Semien 34. The early returns after one season favor the Rangers, as Nimmo has fared better in Texas than Semien has in New York. It was a vastly different situation than the one the Phillies face with Turner, but it's proof of concept that trades like this can happen.

With that in mind, earlier this week Ken Rosenthal of The Athletic reported that the Rangers may be interested in another similar type of trade, as they are looking to move Corey Seager. The two-time World Series MVP will turn 33 in April and is still owed $155M over the next five seasons. Seager underperformed last season but was still worth 3.1 WAR with a 112 OPS+. However, the major caveat with Seager is that he has missed a chunk of time with injuries in each of the last four seasons, as he's averaged only 112 games played every year since 2023. Despite this, he's still averaged 5.4 WAR over the same span, suggesting he is still a productive player in the time he is actually on the field. But he had a career low .736 OPS in 102 games last season, perhaps signifying the beginning of a decline in his bat.

In a world where the Phillies were to work out a Turner for Seager trade, the Rangers would be getting a player who is one year older, less productive, and who is signed for two years longer but has largely remained healthy over the last five years. The Phillies meanwhile would perhaps be getting a better bat and defender at shortstop for a shorter term but with the large risk that said bat along with Seager's overall health may get much worse in the coming years. It's not an ideal swap for either team, but it hardly ever is in trades of this type.

Again, it is highly unlikely that a trade for Turner manifests itself. The most likely scenario is that the Phillies are stuck with him and have no choice but to hope he finds a way to improve. But in a world where a trade was possible, would you be willing to do a bad contract for bad contract trade? What other hypothetical trades would you consider?

#2854. From Jell-O to Philadelphia cream cheese, Kraft Heinz is putting a new spin on old favorites

Kraft Heinz is giving some of America's most familiar grocery staples an unexpected makeover in a bid to revive long-slumping sales and win back support from Wall Street.

The packaged food giant — known for brands like Heinz, Kraft, Philadelphia Cream Cheese, Lunchables and Oscar Mayer — is reinventing its decades-old brands to appeal to changing consumer tastes, particularly growing demand for protein and convenient foods, CEO Steve Cahillane told CNBC's Jim Cramer on Thursday.

"I think the mistake that was made at Kraft Heinz before I arrived in the last several years is this notion that there's a 'forever brand,' that brands can just live on the history of their past," Cahillane said after Kraft Heinz held an investor day earlier Thursday. "That's not true. But equally, brands that existed for a long time are not necessarily destined for the dustbin of history."

"You have to innovate. You have to bring new news. You have to bring excitement with your brand," he added.

That philosophy is central to Cahillane's turnaround strategy. Since taking the helm at the start of 2026, he has directed an additional $700 million toward the business and hired 400 employees. He also paused plans to split Kraft Heinz into two companies. At Thursday's investor day, Kraft Heinz projected that it would return to top- and bottom-line growth in the second half of 2027. The company's revenue has declined on an annual basis in back-to-back years, with analysts also projecting a 1% drop in 2026, according to FactSet.

"I saw a lot that was fixable," Cahillane said. "If we spent all of this year separating the business ... we would have ended up with two suboptimal businesses."

He argued that the company's resources could be put to better use revitalizing the company's brands.

One of the clearest examples of that strategy is Kraft Mac & Cheese. The nearly century-old brand has returned to growth following the introduction of products including PowerMac, which packs 17 grams of protein and six grams of fiber, and Restaurant Edition, which brings restaurant-inspired flavors to the classic boxed format.

Perhaps an unexpected innovation is Kraft Mac & Cheese Cheesy Ramen, which debuted in Canada in July to overwhelming demand. "We sold out three months' worth of stock in a week," Cahillane said, adding that the company plans to bring the cheesy noodles to U.S. shelves next year.

Jell-O is also joining the protein craze, which has been fueled in part by fitness influencers and the rise of GLP-1 weight-loss medications. Kraft Heinz plans to introduce pudding with 10 grams of protein, giving the nostalgic dessert a nutritional boost. Cahillane said the company's research and development team worked to overcome the taste and texture challenges that often come with adding protein to familiar foods.

"Our R&D team has cracked the code," he said. "The chocolate's my favorite. Chocolate Jell-O pudding with protein, I think, is going to be a real hit."

Philadelphia cream cheese is next in line for a protein-packed makeover, following the introduction of new flavors of the regular spread in September. Cahillane said Kraft Heinz plans to introduce new products under the brand next year. "We've got a big opportunity to drive protein, to steal some share from cottage cheese, and that's going to come through the Philadelphia name," he said.

Kraft Heinz shares have struggled for years, but investors are showing signs of renewed optimism. The stock has gained 7% this quarter, outperforming the broader market, even as its roughly flat performance for the year trails the S&P 500's 11% advance.
